CHAPTER I. 
UNDER THE COTTONWOODS, 


whose shining leaves cast 
grateful shade over a 
parklike space of the 
bench land on which stood 
the Jim Keeler ranch 
house, two men paced. The gnarled 
hand of the elder fell onto the strong 
shoulder of the younger, as he said: 

“Hazard, be good t’ Chloe.” 

“Sure. Why not?” the younger re- 
plied with easy assurance that made the 
other frown lightly. 

“Chloe is a quiet, easy-tempered girl, 
if yo’ use her right. But she’s got her 
own mind, Hazard.” 


PINDER the cottonwoods, 


I'm hep to all that, old-timer.” 

“She’s dressing now for her weddin’ 
journey. Her ma's puttin’ on her 
things, as if she was only a baby again. 
Hazard, I want that yo’ should listen 
a minute.” 

The younger man could not conceal 
all his impatience at this lecturing mood 
of the father of the girl he was to wed 
that day, but he controlled his feelings 
with the thought: “After all, I won’t 
have t’ stand this always.” 

“Hazard,” old Jim went on, “for the 
past few years yo’ve acted fine. Be- 
fore that yo’ were wild and scarey. I 
think yo’re over a lot of fool ideas, but 
there’s one think that if a man has in 
him, it will come out. That’s a mean 
temper. Vo've got one, only the last 


4 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


few years yo’ve kept it bottled up. Vo'r 
daddy whopped yo’ many times for 
‘showing it off, and he was my friend. 
He wanted yo’ t’ marry Chloe. I wanted 
it, and_ma did, too. The girl never 
really cottoned t yo’, but yo’ve turned 
out a proper suitor and have caught her 
fancy, it seems. Anyway, she’s set the 
day, and this is it. Vo're goin’ t' town 
t? be married. But save up on that 
temper of yo’rs. Don't try t' work any 
of it off onto her, or she'll quit yo’, 
sure’s shootin’, and she’ll always have 
a home here, if she does.” 

Hazard Prime shrugged his strong 
shoulders and laughed. “Why, Uncle 
Jimmie, I haven’t got mad in so long 
that I sometimes wonder if I could.. I 
won’t get mad at the prettiest girl in 
Alamanzo County, anyway.” 

“I hope not. Of course, an ordinary 
spoutin’ spell, from man or. woman, is 
t' be expected oncet in a while, maybe 
twice in a while, Hazard. What I’m 
referrin’ t are those spells yo’ used t 
have—beating kids younger than yo’r- 
self, breaking hosses with dagger spurs 
and Spanish bits, whipping yo’r dog 
with the leash chain, and all those cruel, 
mean fits of downright temper.” 

Hazard laughed again. “Those were 
just kiddish bursts of rage, like a young 
bronc gets when he’s fightin’ on the bit 
and won’t be broke. Afterward, when 
he gets so he’ll eat pie out of the bust- 
er’s hand, he’s over that.” 

“But yo’ve never been busted wide 
open yet and yo’ve never et pie out of 
any one’s hand.” There was a plaintive 
note in his rich, deep voice. 

„Haven't I? Then maybe Chloe will 
do the breaking.” 

Hazard started toward the ranch 
house on the shoulder of land above 
them. A path ran zigzagging up that 
slope, and at the upper end of that path 
a girl stood, with her mother. 

The girl was womanly and strong, 
with the strength of the rolling out- 
lands. Chloe Keeler had been brought 


up on the JK Ranch, had ridden since 
earliest girlhood, had made a hand, and 
was well qualified to work there, in- 
doors or out. Now, in her modest gray 
traveling dress, blue hat with its side 
bow of pink ribbon in which were 


-sewed a fan of eagle feathers, she made 


a picture to hold the gaze of any man. 
Her mother, beside her, smiled at eager 
Hazard Prime, who advanced with out- 
stretched hands to greet the girl who 
had promised to wed him this day. He 
cried: 

“Chloe—yo’re wonderful, with those 
blue eyes shining and those dusk-rose 
cheeks glowing with health. Never 
mind yo’r ma and pa; give me a kiss.” 

Her kiss was proper, his overwarm. 
They drew apart. Ma Keeler pointed 
to a suit case, and her husband lifted 
it into the back of the wagon in which 
the couple were to drive to Chuckawalla, 
where they were to be married at noon. 

“Don’t be worried if we're not back 
right soon,” Prime called back, as Chloe 
and he got in the wagon. “We may 
stay on the coast longer than we had 
planned. I want t’ give Chloe a real 
good time before we get t’ be old mar- 
ried folks.” 

With a flourish of the whip, the two 
were off, the light wagon swinging to 
and fro on the dim trail leading toward 
the main Chuckawalla road. 

“Well, Chloe, we’re off toward happi- 
ness, at last?’ He put a proprietary 
arm about her as the wagon struck: into 
a rut and bounced out. 

Prime jerked on the reins savagely. 


“Curse those broncs, showin’ off that a 


way, just when I was goin’ t’ ask for 
another kiss.” 

Don't jerk their bits so, Hazard.” 
She put out a restraining hand toward 
his strong right that was twitching the 
reins pitilessly. 

“Oh, them wagon broncs ain’t no 
angels, Chloe,” he said, not moderating 
his treatment of them. The horses 
slowed down, ceased their dancing, 


FOR BETTER OR WORSE 5 


seemed to cringe under the whip that 
flashed over their brown backs. Then 
he let up the pressure of cutting bit and 
put the whip back into its holder with a 
slam. 

“We never have trouble with them,” 
she said, breathing easier as the whip 
was put away. “We drive them int’ 
town, day in and day out, and they 
never make no fuss.” 

“Well, they’ve got t’ learn who's boss 
right off pronto. They’re part of our 
outfit now and they’ve got t’ do as I 
say.” l 

They're mine, not yo’rs. Dad gave 
them t me for part of my wedding 
present.” Firm lines showed around 
her chin, and her eyes had a warning 
glint in them that he did not see, for 
he was scowling at the speeding wagon 
broncs. a 

“Tt’s all the same now,” he said, with 
another proprietary crooking of the arm 
on her side. 

Again the wagon struck a rut and 
bounced, and the broncs, finding the 
reins let loose at the jar, danced and 
snorted. 

“Tl jerk their tongues doi their 
throats.” 

Again the whip leaped forth to 
punish. The horses steadied down at 
the sound of the whip’s rattle, and once 
more the girl saved the horses from 
punishment. The young man relaxed 
a bit. “Chloe, yo’r heart is sure tender. 
But that’s not the way t’ use animals. 
Use them hard and make them know 
who’s the boss. That’s my rule.” 

“Hazard, yo’ know yo’r weakness. I 
promised I'd marry yo’ because I 
thought that wild temper of yo'rs was 
tamed.” 

“I guess it is. Makin’ love t' a girl 
that’s so good, would tame any one’s 
wild temper.” 

He claimed a kiss that was not balked 
by any plunging of the horses or bounc- 
ing of the wagon. The incident seemed 
closed. They chatted now, carefree, the 


horses going steadily after their first 
frisky period. As the main road to 
Chuckawalla came in sight, they swung 
into it. 

“We'll get into town about ten, have 
time t' do our errands and get ready. 
Then we'll be married at noon, take 
the train, and disappear.” He mapped 
out their program. 

She nodded and laughed with a little 
catch of happiness in her throat. “It 
seems too good to be true, that it’s our 
wedding day.” 

“Sure does —his eyes were on her 
in warm admiration—“I suppose the 
wedding day means more ť a girl than 
to a fellow?“ 

“It ought t’ mean the same t' both.“ 

“Oh, in fact, yes, but women are 
more sentimental, aren't they?“ 

“I never got t the rights of that. 
Some women are, and some men are— 
well rather i 

“Rather mushy and soft, eh?” 

The trail swung down into a deep 
cut, with steep walls of red, hard earth 
on either hand. In the middle of the 
trail a coiled threat of mottled terror 
basked. The wagon broncs saw him, 
scented . him—leaped. They cleared 
him, escaping the flashing dart of the 
wedge-shaped head; the hind off wheel 
passed over the threshing coils. The 
wagon broncs danced. 

Hazard Prime swung to his feet be- 
fore the seat of the swaying wagon, 
the reins wrapped around his strong, 
wiry wrists. The cruel bite of the bits 
made the broncs cower; the flash of 
the cutting lash found them all but 
crouched to receive their. punishment. 

“Jumpin’ that a way without any 
cause a-tall,” he cried as the lash whis- 
tled and fell, making dark welts on 
the brown hips. “Ornery, mean, dev- 
ilish 

“Hazard” mer girl’s voice was firm 

they had a reason. A rattler was 
in the trail, and they jumped over it.” 

“No soft-soapin’ t’ save these broncs.

What’re yo’ tryin’ t' do, make a softie 
out of me?“ His temper was boiling 
up fiercely, They're just plumb loco, 
that’s all, and I'll take it out of them.” 
Again the iron wrists worked and 
sawed at the cutting bits; the lash 
whizzed again to leave its mark on heav- 
ing flanks and far up toward the quiv- 
ering withers. 

‘“Hazard—I beg yo’ll not cut those 
hosses that a way. They have been my 
pets for so long.” 

They're mine now, curse them!” 
His voice broke on a note of high 
frenzy. “Mine, an’ yo're mine. Shut 
up and let me use them like they ought 
t' be used. There they go, dancing 
again.” The broncs, having indeed been 
hauled backward, were now over the 
‘threshing, maimed coils of the wheel- 
crushed rattler. “TI’ll show them whose 
they are.” 

His fury mounted with opposition. 
She tried to put her hands on the reins. 
He struck her hand aside with one of 
his fists, the one that was clenched 
about the whip’s butt. The. blow hurt 
her softer hand, crumpling back the fin- 
gers, paining the tendons in her wrist 
and forearm. 

“Hazard, yo’re beside yo'rself with 
blind rage. Wait, calm down, and it'll 
be better soon.” She tried to wheedle 
him into a more humane mood. 

“Shut up! Do as I say an’ keep yo’ 
hands off those reins. PI kill these 
broncs but I’ll master them. They’re 
mine now, and yo’re mine.“ 

She shrank down in the seat as he 
stood up again, brandishing the whip. 
The whip rose and fell; the bits cut 
back against the tender mouths. The 
girl leaped to her feet and seized the 
reins, trying to stop him in his orgy of 
punishment. 

He kept the reins, thrusting at her 
savagely with the point. of his stiffened 
elbow, sending her reeling. She wotld 
have fallen out of the wagon, to the 
hard road, had she not turned in plung- 


ing and thus toppled over the seat, 
where she caught herself by one hand 
that struck the suit cases tied there. 
The horses danced anew, and the whip 
rose and fell. She slid off the back of 
the wagon onto the threshing coils that 
had at their tip a buzzing. The crushed 
head had lost power to harm, luckily 
for the stunned girl. She saw the 
horses cease their plunging, saw Hazard 
Prime swing down, letting the reins 
catch around the whipstock. The horses 
stood and shivered. He came to her, 
caught her erect roughly, his face black 
with rage. 

“What did yo’ do that for? I won't 
have this baby-play stuff. Get into that 
wagon.” ` 

The girl, numbed by the shock of th 
fall and the sudden revelation of his 
character, let herself be shoved into the 
wagon seat. Her eyes took in the de- 
tails of the cuts on the horses’ sides and 
withers. She saw the crimson. on the 
bits as the horses champed at them 
nervously. 

“They were frightened by that rat- 
tler she began. 

“Yo lie! There wasn’t any rattler. 
Keep still. Were late now, and I’m 
goin’ t' make up for lost time,” he mut- 
tered, jerking the reins and so bringing 
the horses to attention. 

He laid on the lash well, and the ani- 
mals started down the road rapidly, 
their hips shrinking down every time 
he flourished the whip. He laughed. 

“That’s the way. Make animals and 
people step along t' yo’r music. See 
them go now at the mere sound of the 
whip. That’s the way.” 

“It may be the way with dumb brutes 
but it won’t be the way with me,” the 
girl said steadily. 

“Oh, forget that.” He laughed with 
easy resonance. We'll have spats, 
same's the old folks did. I wouldn't 
ever cut yo’ t’ ribbons, Chloe.” He 
moved closer to her and she shrank 
from him.

“Oh, don't be squeamish. Give me 
a kiss?” 

“No.” She turned her head away. 

“By Heaven, yo’ will!” he exclaimed, 
with reins in one hand, the other reach- 
ing out to grasp her by the shoulder and 
thus pull her toward him. k 

She evaded him by twisting her body 
and leaning far out over the end of the 
seat, over the wheel. He cursed under 


his breath and drew back, his face a. 


mask of fury. 

I'm glad I saw yo’ once this a way,” 
Chloe said firmly. ‘It shows off yo’r 
real character that yo’ve been hiding.” 

“Oh, does it? It’s pretty late t' be 
findin' out all these things.“ 

Pretty late, but not too late,“ she 
said, her hands going to the reins again. 

She spoke to the horses that stopped 
at once at a remembered command from 
her own lips and stood, twitching their 
ears forward and back expectantly for 
another order from her. 

“What does this mean?” he cried, 
choking back a new surge of rage. 

“It's pretty late but not too late— 
adios.” - 

She leaped to the trail, while he stared 
at her in unbelief. 

Chloe what do yo’ mean?” 

“Yo’ve shown yo'rse'f for what yo’ 
are mean, sullen, abusive and unkind. 
Adios.” 7 

“Meaning?” 

“Tl walk to town. 
the horses in a stable. 
and drive home—alone.” 

“Then yo're not goin’ t' marry me?” 

“No.” Her voice steady and firm. 

“By Heaven ” Then his arm 
flashed up, the whip sang and only her 
nimble side step saved her from the cut 
of the lash. 

The whip fell on the broncs, and they 
were going down the trail in a cloud of 
dust. But after that first stroke on 
their shrinking flanks, he had not struck 
them again. There was, indeed, no 
need. Sitting humped over in the seat, 


Yo’ can leave

he tooled them around the bend, down

CHAPTER II, 
Nor TOO LATE, l 


THE girl trudged sturdily onward; if 
her shoulder heaved a bit from emo- 
tion, it soon passed off. A road runner 
came into the trail from somewhere and 
paced away from her as if she had been 
standing still. A jack rabbit went off 
to one side of the road like a gray- 
brown streak. She came around the 
bend, topped a final rise, and then be- 
gan to trudge down into Chuckawalla. 
But Chloe lacked the courage, then, 
to face people. Every one in town 
knew of their engagement, and not a 
few had advance knowledge that this 
was to have been their wedding day. 
The girl had expected that friends of 
Hazard Prime would be at the train 
to give them a noisy send-off. Not.a 
few of her own friends lived in town 
and would want to see the bride, after 
Prime and she had visited the minister, 
she reflected. 

Instead of going to the town’s center, 
Chloe Keeler turned into a side street, 
went up that, turned into another, and 
found what she sought—the Chucka- 
walla Park, a group of fast-growing 
cottonwoods and other trees and shrubs, 
watered by a bend of the creek that 
flowed through the little valley. There 
were walks and a few seats and a dance 
floor with a band stand. Chuckawalla 


was proud of her park and justly so. 


The girl slumped down on one of the 
seats and began to go over the wreck 
of her hopes. It had been a wrench to 
refuse to go through with the marriage 
ceremony, but she was surprised to find 
that it was less of a break now than she 
had thought it would turn out to be. “He 
tried t' lash me with that whip. If he 
did that as a bridegroom, what would 
he do as a married man? It was pretty 


8 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


late to have savin’ thoughts, but it 
wasn’t too late, thank Heaven!” 

She did not want to see any one but 
sat there quietly. It was an hour when 
the villagers would not be much in the 
park. She wanted to be alone, to think 
matters out. Suddenly she started 
partly erect, on her face a slight frown 
of disappointment. Coming toward her, 
through the little park, was a man. 

He was dressed in range clothes— 
plain chaps, wide-brim hat, with a neat 
knotted neck cloth of blue and white 
check. It was clean, and the white 
checks in it were white. He had on a 
well-fitting suit of moleskin and nice, 
new boots; his spurs showed a touch of 
silver on the shank. One gun swung 
low on his left thigh. 

“Another man, a puncher, cruel to 
hosses, cruel to cows, mean to every 
one,” the girl thought. She shrank 
down behind the back of her seat, as 
if she were stretched partly out, to rest. 
The man, his head high and his feet 
swinging fast, did not see her. As he 
looked at the yellow paper in his hand, 
he went toward another seat a few 
paths away from her own and quickly 
sat. down. : 

The girl watched him furtively. “He 
looks as if he were in trouble, too, but 
his trouble isn’t a patch on mine,” she 
thought. “I hope he doesn’t notice me 
and try t’ flirt or anything. I’ve had 
enough of men for one day.” 

‘It was pretty late, but it wasn’t too 
late! 

The man on that other bench was not 
trying to flirt with Chloe Keeler; in 
fact, he did not notice her. He seemed 
lost in contemplation of the slip of yel- 
low paper in his hand. He read it, re- 
read it, turned it, and twisted it, as if 
to get from it another meaning. 

The young fellow, Chloe noted, while 
not handsome, was not ill looking. He 
was of medium height, seemed in fine 
health. His hair had a tinge of red in 
its brown tones. His shoulders were 


square. His hands, as he raised them 
from time to time in unconscious ges- 
tures of despair, were white, telling of 
gloves carefully worn in his everyday 
work, a mark of neatness the girl could 
appreciate. Their light color, contrast- 
ing with the tan of his face, told of 
habitual use of gloves to protect the 
hands from the cut of rope and chafing 
of bridle reins. Finally he stuffed the 
offending paper in his pocket and sat 
more erect. He plunged both hands 
deep into his trousers pockets, stretched 
out his slender limbs, yawned, looked 
about, half got up, then settled back into 
another phase of his black mood. 

Chloe saw him suddenly stiffen; then 
he jerked out his gun and held it bal- 
anced in his hand, the wrist loose. The 
gun seemed to waver, then it swung up, 
up, up, toward his head. “Heavens, 
he’s going to commit suicide,” was the 
girl’s instinctive thought. 

Like a flash she rolled off her bench 
and scurried toward him, arriving, with- 
out sound, behind him in time to see 
that the muzzle of the gun was in line 
with his right eye. 

“Don’t—don’t!” She struck the gun 
aside, 

The gun fell clatteringly to the grav- 
eled walk at his feet. She sprang 
around the end of the bench, planting 
one shapely foot on the gun. 

“How dare yo’ point that gun at 
yo’rself that a way?” she cried, panting 
from the dynamic effort she had made 
in thus reaching him hurriedly. “No 
man ought t’ try t’ kill himself, no mat- 
ter what the cause.” 

His level brown eyes came to rest on 
her flushed face. The roses were flam- 
ing on her rounded cheeks now, and her 
eyes were blazing with excitement. The 
tides of life in her, that had run so low 
when she had entered the town with her 
romance shredded to tatters, were again 
in full power. The sensations of the 
moment had aroused her interest in 
affairs mundane, and her woman’s heart 


FOR BETTER OR WORSE 9 


of pity had been touched by the dis- 
tress of another. 

“Td like my gun, if yo’ please, miss.” 
His voice was full and resonant. 

“Yo’ cain't have it.“ She stamped 
her pretty boot on it. 

He bent over, something like a smile 
showing on his face. She kept her foot 
firm on the gun. He took hold of the 
gun and tugged at it, but her strong 
body’s weight was on it. 

“Miss, please move.” 

“And have yo’ kill yo’self with it?” 

“No, ma’am, I was only looking in 
the barrel t’ see if there was sand in 
it. I dropped it coming into town; 
shook out of my holster as my hoss 
slid down a cut bank that was mostly 
sand. I wanted t’ make sure the barrel 
wouldn’t lead, with sand in it that a 
way, before I had t’ use it again.” 

“How do I know yo’re not lyin’?” 
Her foot was still on the gun. 

He continued to bend over, tugging 
at the gun gently. “Yo’ll have t' take 
my word for it. That's all.” 

“I won't let yo’ have it.” 

“Yes, yo’ will—there.” 

He had grasped her ankle, given it a 
tug and secured the gun. Her cheeks 
now flamed with something akin to 
anger. 

“Sir * 

“I beg pa' don, ma’am, but yo’ made 
me do it,” he said contritely. His hat 
came off, and he stood erect. “I beg 
yo’ pa’don, ma’am. I’m all upset to- 
day and I guess I’ve made a fool of 
myself. My name is Len Burris, of 
the L Bar B over near Goosetown.” 

The amusing side of it all then struck 
her, and she put back her pretty head 


and laughed. A rather forlorn grin’ 


came to Len’s face, as he holstered the 
gun with smooth, supple play of wrist 
and hand. She was turning away when 
he spoke again. 

“Excuse me, ma’am, but do yo’ live 
here?” 


“No. On the JK Ranch near town; 


Jim Keeler’s ranch. I am his daughter, 
Chloe.”

This man could take care of a woman

safe. 

“It’s a risk—for us both. We know 
nothing of each other. But I’ll go with 
yo’, Len Burris.” She held out her 
loyal hand. And we'll see what the 
world has in store for us—together.”

about making camp. She joined her 
efforts to his. He praised her, and she 
found herself flushing with pleasure at 
his praise. When supper was over and 
the things made tidy, he unrolled her 
blankets, spreading them in a tiny de- 
pression between two big stones, after 
he had filled the depression with aro- 
matic dried stuff gathered near by.

my blankets down where the hosses are 
hobbled—to make sure they don’t stray 
too far. That all right?” 

“Thanks—very much,” she said, and 
then he strode away into the gathering 
gloom. ~ 

Chloe’s thoughts were in a tumult. 
Out of the disorder of her thoughts 
emerged these that were dominant over 
all the others: 

“He kept his word; he’s kind to ani- 
mals; he is kind to me. I wonder if 
Hazard Prime meant anything by say- 
ing, ‘when yo’ find out what he’s wanted 
for?” 

“He kept his word—he was kind to 
me. Hazard Prime has a big. ranch, 
and this man may not have anything, in 


a money way, but he was kind to me 
when I needed it—most!” 

Then she slept with a strange, com- 
forting trust in this man who had come 
so strangely into her life.

CHAPTER IV. 
THE OTHER GIRL. 


NGRY and chopfallen, Hazard 

Prime went down the street with 
his cronies. With his reputation for 
being able to fight with fists or weapons, 
Prime had cowed most of the men he 
associated with and won the respect of 
the others. He had a large following 
among the “sporty” element of the 
town. Known to play a fast game of 
pocket billiards, he was hard to beat 
at stud or California Jack. He was 
the leader in all the town merrymak- 
ings, yet he had managed to keep his 
sporty proclivities concealed from old 
Jim Keeler and Chloe, considering him- 
self sly in thus deceiving them. 

As he walked up the street with his 
friends, after Chloe and Len Burris had 
gone out of town, he felt, curiously, a 
sense of relief at the thought that he 
would no longer have to “play the milk- 
sop,” as he termed it. 

„I'm glad she didn’t marry me,” he 
told the crowd confidentially; she is 
a too-proper piece of calico. I always 
had to be just so with Chloe Keeler, 
boys. No life to her. I'd not been 
able t' keep with the gang if Pd mar- 
ried her not without a row.“ 

“Sure, yo're better off not being a 
bridled groom,” Hector Wiles agreed 
with his long-time friend. 

“Sure, Heck! I'll cut loose now, and

got foolish and been married. Only 
this will be a stag party, with the accent 
on the stag.” 

They laughed and backed him up in 
his intention to make merry. One 
hinted: “Why didn’t yo’ fork a hoss 
and go after them, Haz?”

CHAPTER VI.

SPEEDING northward, side by side,

needs of the rough route forbade con- 


nected talk., The girls pulses raced

to talk,

ty-four hours? True, he was her hus-

two-dollar bills. She had been given

her gloves back in Goosetown! She 
knew nothing of the man, after all.

break for the open this a way?”

chatter.

If the intense cold -did not speedily 
come to an end, their situation would 
For three 
days and nights the premature winter 
blizzard had raged, catching them in 
the obscure cañon of the high country 
and fairly walling them in with snow. 
The latter lay yards deep all around the 
cabin. Their provisions -had been low 
when the storm struck; before the suc-

32 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


the high country can produce—they 
were gone. 

It was only mid-October, however. 
There was a chance that the kindly 
“chinook” would come along to warm 
the air and melt down the appalling 
drifts, enabling the marooned men 
to escape. Unless something of the 
sort happened they were certainly 
doomed; for their ammunition, as well 
as their food supplies, was exhausted. 
In the meantime the imperative need 
was for food. Actual starvation men- 
aced them. Never the mildest of in- 
dividuals under the most favorable con- 
ditions, Big Tom Shields had reverted 
to the primitive with the encroachment 
of desperate hunger. At this moment 
he was half insane. Mike Hughes’ stub- 
born defense of an ideal—fidelity to a 
dog—might have been considered fool- 
hardy under the circumstances. 

As the two men sat on their bunks 
and watched each other, the eerie howl 
drew closer. Were the wolves indeed 
chasing a luckless deer? It seemed 
likely. And the two prospectors swal- 
lowed convulsively at the thought of the 
succulent venison that would probably 
be disappearing down the throats of 
the gray bandits of the mountains. The 
cafion widened out considerably at a 
little distance below the cabin, but the 
sides remained steep. If they did not 
catch their quarry before they passed, 
it might even be possible to watch them 
as they. flashed past. 

Knowing that he had nothing to fear 
from his diminutive companion, Big 
Tom was the first to act upon the 
thought. He moved to the door and 
took down the bar from the cleats. The 
howls rose louder and louder. Pres- 
ently he opened the door a crack and 
peered out. 

The moon threw a bright glare, al- 
most that of early morning, over the 
scene. Directly in front of the cabin 
the wind had blown a depression be- 
tween two great drifts, giving a widen- 


ing perspective to any one looking from 
the doorway. As Big Tom peered, a 
deer flashed gracefully into his field of 
vision, springing up and down in the 
deep snow, running as if almost ex- 
hausted. 

“Look at that, will you?“ 

At the exclamation Mike Hughes 
crowded close to his companion and 
looked out.. As he did so a gray shape, 
evidently the foremost of the pursuing 
animals, shot into view closely behind. 
The deer had landed in a drift of un- 
usual depth; before it could extricate 
itself, the gray shape had launched it- 
self upon the graceful flanks, bringing 
the quarry to a sudden halt. 

So close to the cabin did it happen 
that the watchers could distinctly hear 
the snap of the wolves’ jaws as they 
swarmed over the stricken deer. In an 
instant they were embroiled in a ter- 
rific battle, their snarls rising on the 
night air in blood-curdling fashion. 
Snow was flying in every direction. 

Ain't this fierce?” said Big Tom 
with an oath. The two men were for 
the moment unconscious of the fearful 
cold in the engrossment supplied by the 
fight. “All that venison out there, an’ 
not a shell to shoot them wolves with. 
Dang their ugly hides! If I had——” 

At that moment the biggest of the 
fighting animals, its shape startlingly 
distinct in the moonlight, leaped at the 
throat of another and, with a single 
ferocious snap, put it out of the run- 
ning. The other two were fighting des- 
perately a few feet away. The victor 
leaped upon the struggling pair and 
bore them to the snow, snapping indis- 
criminately right and left. In ten sec- 
onds there was but one Jeft to fight. 
The ensuing duel left nothing to be de- 
sired in the way of cold ferocity, while 
the carcass of the deer lay in the snow 
a short distance away. 

“Only one left!” exclaimed Big Tom 
a minute later, in high excitement. 
“Gimn.e one o’ them knives—quick !”

Which was the big man’s way of re-

Synopsis of Preceding Chapters 


ANDY and Ross Hale are raising their boys in competition with each other. 
has gone East for seven years of school and college. 


Peter, Ross’ boy, 
Charlie has stayed on the range. Upon 


Ross’ return, Will Nast, the ‘sheriff, is to decide which is the finer person. 
Andy amasses wealth and holdings, while Ross is forced to sell most of his belongings and 


borrow. But Peter’s athletic fame compensates for his father’s poverty. 
at college, Peter’s amazing career in all sports comes to a mysterious end. 
Upon his return home, three years later, it is all explained. 


After his freshman year 


For, despite his enormous 


strength, Peter, through an accident, has become a cripple, forced to use .crutches, which he 


manipulates with superhuman skill. 


After Ross gets over his first moments of despair at his son’s plight, he learns that Charlie is 
announcing his engagement to Ruth McNair, the daughter of an extremely wealthy rancher. 


Ross had hoped Peter would marry her.

In his. own room, he went over ev-

been when he left. All was in good 
order, too. There was no sense of 
sticky mold and damp about the cham- 
ber, as there usually is in a room which 
has not been lived in for a long time. 
Instead, there was a sweetness in the 
atmosphere that proved this room had 
been cleaned and aired with some regu- 
larity. It told Peter everything that he 
could have asked. 

Still he made a slow round of the 
rest of the old ranch house. It was 
like moving through the bare ribs of 
a building which has been wrecked by 
fire. He could remember this house in 
the old days as a veritable bower of 
coolness in summer and of warmth in 
winter, but this was now all changed, 
for the trees which had once shaded 
and beautified the. old heuse were all 
gone, and he did not need to be told 
where the bodies of them had gone.

They had been transmuted into text

arms of Ross Hale seemed strangely 
weak on this day. Peter took the ax 
from him without a word. 


CHAPTER IX. 


PIPE SMOKE. 
CAN you swing an ax, too?” asked 
Ross Hale. 

“Watch me!” 

It was a novel sight to see Peter 
support himself, driving the iron end 
of his brace into the ground to anchor

hands. From that tough wood the tool 
had been rebounding impotently while 
Ross Hale swung it. But now that 
Peter stood to the work, all was 
changed. The very first blow fleshed 
the ax by half the depth of its blade; 
the second brought out a chip as big 
as the joined hands of a man. And 


the ringing of every stroke echoed far. 


off, like the rhythmic explosions of a 
long rifle. ; 

Ross Hale, watching with wonder,

had not been maimed—— 

“Ts that enough?” asked Peter. 

The butt of the big log had been

here was Peter, resting lightly on the

looked at his father, but there was too 
much pride and pain combined in the

which had disabled him, must have 
bent his mind seriously and scientifi-

substitutes which remained to him, 
namely, a good set of wits and his 
giant strength above the hips. He had 
used his athletic training to prepare 
himself, and he had used his trained 
brain to.study the problems and the 
way to master them. Now he went 
with faultless accuracy up the kitchen 
steps, supporting a mountainous load 
of wood such as Ross Hale himself 
could never have managed, 

Peter led the way into the darken-

sus before the stove and cooked the

Another thought came to Ross Hale,

which his homecoming had produced,

as cheerful! 

The tension increased every moment, 
until it seemed to Ross Hale that he 
could not endure it. Finally he de- 
cided to make his escape. The instant 
supper was ended—such as it was—he 
accepted Peter’s offer to do the dishes 
and went out to walk wp and down in

Behind him, in the kitchen of the 
house, he could hear Peter’s voice

through the old house. But the father 
knew there was only a pretended cheer 
behind that singing. The soul of 
Peter, in reality, was burdened under 
a greater. weight of sorrow than Ross 
Hale himself could feel. 

The dishwashing and drying pro-

creaking as his father’s heels ground

It seemed to Peter that smoking

It had been a sort of crucifixion. Not

father of a man who might never be 
self-supporting! 

Peter, in his agony of mind, took his

and his hands. There was a sharp, 
cracking sound, a sting on the palm of 
his hand and the ball of his thumb. 
He had broken his favorite and only 
Pipe into a thousand pieces! 

He did not curse, but, looking at 
the fuming little ruin that had fallen 
on the floor, he wondered what other 
man’s hand could have crushed the 
stout brier root in that pipe as he had 
with thumb and forefinger. 
Feeling a sudden need for the open

father’s revolvers, where Ross Hale 
had left them on the kitchen table. 
Peter stopped and picked them up. 
They were good guns. In the Hale 
family, the men had always been proud 
of their weapons. And these fitted 
neatly against the heel of Peter’s palm. 
He slipped them into his coat and 
went on.

CHAPTER X. 
A HOLDUP ARTIST. 


OT even a cat, no doubt, could 

have covered the distance to the 
side gate without making some noise 
among the cinders which lined the path 
—to keep down dust.in summer and 
mud in winter. But Peter moved on 
crutches shod with broad, air-cush- 


40 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


ioned feet of rubber. And these made 
uot so much as a whisper as he 
teached the gate. 

He passed through it with care. 
Then he swung himself off into the 
darkness of the corral. His mind was 
fairly clear as to the purpose before 
him. If only he could do the thing and 
be back again before his father got into 
the house and missed the guns. 

In the barn, he took the better of the 


two horses—at least, it was the one 


which appeared toughest and most able 
to bear Peter’s crushing weight. He 
saddled and bridled this cartoon of a 
horse. Since his accident he had spent 
many an hour in the saddle, and there- 
fore he knew just how to manage ev- 
erything. He knew the whole trick of 
mounting and he knew just how to 
manage himself, once he climbed into 
the saddle. 

First, when he was settled, he 
strapped his legs into the stirrups. He 
arranged the braces on either side, and 
then he was ready, being able to ride 
in this fashion as well as another 
man, and with this danger only—that 
if the horse fell and rolled with him 
—well, that would be the end of 
Peter! He had grown accustomed to 
taking chances. 

With unsteady canter he was carried 
up the road. As he pushed the tired 
mustang toward the goal, there was no 
sign of a campfire on either hand. 


Perhaps drunken Mike Jarvin had de- 


cided to push on all the night toward 
the quarries, for the impulses which 
moved Jarvin were ever sudden and 
wild. 

For the greater part of an hour he 
drove the mustang steadily up the 
road; then he checked the horse.

CHAPTER XI. 
A FLASH OF HOPE. 


SHEN Peter rode in and found thë 
thing that he wanted, without so 
much as dismounting from his horse. 
It was a little streak in the dust; 
swinging to one side, he scooped up a 
wallet. He pinched it, and a crisp 


Or do you want. 


rustling came forth to greet his- ears. 
Peter could not help smiling through 
the darkness. 

He turned his mustang from the 
road, jogging across the fields and into 
a clump of trees. There he lighted a 
few handfuls of twigs, and by that 
flickering illumination he examined his 
treasure. The belt was loaded with fat 
twenty-dollar gold pieces, laid side by 
side, and it was almost heavy enough 
to make a load in itself. Forty or 
fifty pounds, he reckoned its weight, 
and when he counted the coins he 
found that there were exactly two hun- 
dred and fifty of them. In this divi- 
sion of his capture he had scooped in 
an even five thousand dollars! 

But that was not all. Opening the 
wallet, he found it neatly and thickly 
stacked with bills. It was.no ‘wonder 
that fat Jarvin had surrendered the 
wallet even less willingly than he had 
surrendered the belt with its load of 
gold. That little sheaf of government 
promises to pay totaled’ a full twelve 
thousand dollars! 

Even Peter was a little impressed. 
He counted the money again. Then 
he went through the coat. There was 
a tangle of papers and notes and odds 
and ends. He emptied all of these into 
his saddle bags, to be examined at his 
leisure. As for the coat, he burned it 
in the last of his fire, after which he 
still delayed to stamp out the last em- 
bers, then kicked the ashes and the 
blackend remains of the fire beneath 
the bushes. He threw some quantities 
of pine needles over the spot, mounted 
his mustang, and started back. 

He felt it was more or less his duty 
to look at the. spot where he had left 
the Buttrick brothers lying in the road, 
but a little reflection convinced him 
that Lefty, whom he had thrown down 
with his hand, might have been 
stunned, but he could not have been 
‘seriously injured. And he would re- 
cover and give help to Dan. Even

have been. However, he felt that he 
had struck the first blow, and that 
thereafter the load of gloom would be 
somewhat lifted from the ranch house 
and the people in it. . 

Peter slept that night lightly—a 
broken sleep. And when the first gray 
of the dawn was beginning he left the 
ranch, harnessed the team to the buck- 
board, and drove hastily down the main 
road and then a mile down the twist- 
ing lane that led to the Vincent place. 


It was just the same. Old Tucker 
Vincent was dead, and now Tucker’s 
son looked just as old and as white- 
haired and as dignified as the original 
Tucker had always appeared. The 
county would not have been the county, 
if it had not been for the Vincents, 
because they were a veritable pool, out 
of which good-blooded cattle and 
horses were constantly drawn. They 
were always raising, and they were al- 
ways buying. And they never pro- 
duced and they never bought anything 
but the most perfect stock for the 
range, 

In an hour Peter had done the thing 
for which he came. He had selected 
fifty head of stock, and in the place of 
the two broken-down mustangs there 
were two sound young horses before 
him; four more would be driven over 
with the stock. All these things were 
paid for in crisp, new bank notes, 
taken from a sheaf of a comfortable 
size, Then Peter turned the heads of 
his team toward town. He made his 
new steppers streak down the highway 
till they reached Sumnertown, where he 
made his brief rounds of the stores. 
Filling his. buckboard with all that it 
would carry in the way of provisions, 
he turned back toward the ranch. 

As he came up the road past his fa- 
ther’s house, he saw Ross Hale on the 
front porch, smoking his eternal pipe 
and looking forth at the world through 
eyes which were misted with weariness. 
He came running out and shouting 
when he saw Peter. 

“Pete!” he cried. “Go into the cor- 
ral and tell them fools to drive the 
cows out, will you? They are tryin’ 
to tell me that you been over at Vin- 
cent’s and have ordered fifty whole 
head, to say nothing of four of the 
finest-looking hosses that ever 

He paused here, for his eyes lighted 
upon the new team in front of the 
buckboard and the -heaped packages 
which piled the wagon.

A flash of real hope and desire to 
believe darted like lightning across the

CHAPTER XII. 
A NEW LIFE. 


HEN matters moved for Peter 

they moved very fast. It was not 
many days before the bank had re- 
ceived its money and the little, petty 
debts with tradesmen around Sumner- 
town had been cleared away. There 
then remained nothing between Mr. 
Ross Hale and a new life except the 
habit of sorrow which had fallen upon 
him. 

However, he could not be long in 
being aware of a new existence. For, 
when he wakened in the morning, he 
could hear the hammers, big and little, 
chanting in chorus out beyond the cor- 
ral, where the carpenters were whack- 
ing up a new barn as fast as their 
hands could raise and put together with 
spikes of a healthy size. Moreover, he 
could not fail to take note of the string 
of ten mules which had been brought 
into the pasture.. Ten well-selected 
mules, well fitted for dragging the five- 
share plow through the soil of the 
farming land in the bottom, the sur- 
face of which had not been touched 
these seven years and more. 


There were good tools of all kinds, 


which Peter had collected at the Lef- 
fingwell sale. They had cost nearly 
nothing, and they could be made to 
serve as well as new implements. For 
a true Western farmer will not pay five 
cents on the dollar for rusted imple- 
ments. He feels that rust may cover 
up some mysteriously hidden weakness. 
Vet, for all his dislike of rust, with the 
carelessness of the true exploiter of a 
new country, he scorns to take the 
small expense and trouble of housing 
his tools. 

But Peter was not proud. He made 
his offers against the junk man, and 


he won out at ridiculously low figures. 
He heard his father itemizing his pur- 
chases fer the benefit of Andy Hale, 
when the latter came to call and ask 
the meaning of the strange signs of 
life which were being shown on his 
brother’s ranch. Included among the 
necessary implements, vehicles, cattle, 
and horses, were the entire fittings of 
a blacksmith shop—complete, though 
secondhand—all the other contraptions 
necessary to make the heart of an iron 
worker swell with pride. He had even 
bought a whole young grove of fast- 
growing poplars and such shade mak- 
ers, to be set up in the yard around 
the old ranch house, so that it began 
to look as comfortable as ever. Sun- 
dry oaks and fig trees were set out 
where time would make them thrive 


and they would eventually displace the 


rank poplars. A load of secondhand 


furniture had been secured at the last 


great sale. 

Peter, who had gone to the house 
on his noiseless crutches, sneaked hast- 
ily back into the old shed where he 
had been renovating the secondhand 
furniture. Therefore he could not 
hear the concluding speeches of his 
proud and bewildered father. But 
these had to do with the erection of 
the new barn. : 

“He went over to the Cumberwell

timber was well seasoned and would

what’s happening!”

CHAPTER XIII. 
RUTH M’NAIR. 


BOTH Ross and Andy Hale ad- 
vanced a great deal more slowly 


than they might have done, had they 


wished to avoid overhearing that con- 
versation which was in progress in the 
shed. But as it was they went on al- 
most on tiptoe. Certainly they heard, 
for a reward, the most harmless 
chatter. 

The girl did most of the talking. 
Peter put in- only a word now and 
then. He seemed to be asking her ad- 
vice. It appeared to be a matter of 
the greatest moment, to Peter, to learn 
what she might be able to advise con- 
cerning the manner in which a table, 
which he was sandpapering, might be 
repainted. He wanted to know, also, 
what suggestions she could give him 
concerning color schemes. 


The result was that Ruth McNair 
drew closer and closer until, in the end, 
the rasping of the sandpaper ceased, 
and Peter sat on his crippled legs, with 
Ruth McNair sitting before him, her 
knees clasped in her hands and her 
head thrown back with enthusiasm 
while she uttered her ideas. It was all 
extremely innocent, but it made Andy 
Hale grow black of face. He took his 
brother by the arm and led him hastily 


away. 
“Whats wrong?” asked Ross Hale 
blandly. 
“You know well enough what's 
wrong!” snapped his brother. “You 
know well enough, Ross. It’s the 


thing that’s making you grin so broadly 
just now.” 

“Got no idea in the world what you 
mean!” said Ross. 

“Ross,” said Andy, “lemme tell you 
that you and me have had times when. 
we ain’t been particularly friendly. But 
that ain’t been ,because of what I 
wished. Never wanted anything ex- 
cept to have you for a brother and a 
friend, Ross. But I’ll tell you this: 
Folks that interfere with my best plans 
is my enemies, no matter whether they 
wear the names of brothers—and 
nephews—or not.” 

He said this with such a solemn air 
of significance that Ross Hale looked 
sharply askance at him. “That’s free 
talk and out-and-out talk,” said Ross 
Hale. “But still I’m cursed if I know 
just what you mean!” 

Andy shook his head. “You know 
as well as I know. Your boy is mak- 
ing a dead set at the girl that’s en- 
gaged to marry my boy Charlie!” 

Ross Hale started violently. “It ain’t 
true, Andy!” he cried. 

“Tt is true. And you jumped when 
I said it!” 

“What in the world could put such 
an idea into your head, Andy? Peter 
ain’t ever seen her before to-day!” 
“How many times does a man have 


48 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


to see Ruth McNair before he would 


want her?” snapped Andy. Ain't she 
got looks enough? Ain't her father 
got land and cows and cash enough? 
Answer me that!” 

“Peter ain't a fool!” said his father 
rather weakly. 

“He ain’t a fool!” answered Andy, 
“and that's the main reason why I 
should like to ask you if he ain’t able 
to see that Ruth McNair would make 
a likely wife for any man!” 

“He knows,” said Ross Hale, grow-

“that she’s engaged to his cousin. And 
what chance would a cripple like my 
Peter have against a fine, upstanding 
boy like your Charlie?” 

Andy Hale snarled with anger. 
“You may talk your boy down, but all 
the time you're thinking him up. 
Now, Ross, I’m the last man in the 
world to run down your boy. He's 
got brains and nerve and plain grit and 
a head that’s working all of the time: 
He don’t miss any chances. He's 
showed that already by the way that 
he’s running this ranch for you. But 
he’s showing another thing, too, that 


he’ll throw no tricks away. And one 


of the strongest tricks that he has to 
play with a girl like that is the fact 
that he’s a cripple, Ross. You know 
that. You take a girl like that, and 
when she thinks what a grand athlete 
Peter used to be, and when she sees 
him so broken down now—but so 
hard working and so clever and so 
cheerful—how could she hardly help 
from wanting to mother him?” 

“Andy, you talk through your hat! 
You listened to one minute’s talk be- 
tween them, and you come to a lot of 
fool conclusions that ain’t got nothing 
to do with the truth at all. J heard 
nothing out of the way, and I heard 
everything that you heard.” 

“You heard him asking her advice, 
didn’t you?” 

“What of that? About the colors 


to use on furniture.. A woman knows 
more than a man does about things 
like that!“ 

“Does she? I tell you, Ross, you 
can’t pull the wool over my eyes. 
There ain’t a thing in the world, in- 
cluding color schemes, that Peter Hale 
needs to learn from a snip of a girl 
like Ruth McNair. She’s got a pretty 
face and she’s got money. But you 
know, and I know, that girls don’t 
carry around so much brains with them 
that they can scatter knowledge around 
them like seed behind a plow. No, 
Ross, I tell you that Peter is making 
a dead set at Ruth MoNair, and I don’t 
take it kind of him. You understand 
me? I don’t take it kind of him! 
That girl belongs to his cousin!“ 

What Andy said was true enough, it 
seemed to Ross Hale. And the very 
truth of it made him swell suddenly 
with anger, so that he snapped out: 
“When I was down and miserable and 
done for, Andy, I never got no help 
from you!” 

“What has that got to do with it?” 
cried Andy. “What has that got to do 
with this? And, besides, was there ever 


a time that you asked for help?” 


“Is it for a brother to ask a brother 
for help?” said Ross Hale, fairly quiv- 
ering with a bitter emotion. “You 
knew that I was down and out. I 
didn’t have a coat to my back, hardly. 
I was out of elbow with every shirt 
that I had. There was many and many 
a time when I come back from my 
days work and sat down in that 
kitchen, yonder, and chewed at a stale 
crust of bread. I tell you, I’ve gone 
down to the bottom of the bread box 
and took out an old heel and cut away 
the moldy part of it and chewed up 
the rest. I’ve done that. And you 
knew it! Tell me, didn’t you know it?” 

“How was I to guess what you was 
doing, Ross?” asked Andy Hale. “I 
knew that you had enough money to 
keep your boy back East in an expen- 


ACRES OF UNREST 49 


sive way and pay for his fine clothes 
and his tuition and all the rest, that 
he was spending like water! Should I 
guess that any man would spend ev- 
erything en a——” 

“You seen me cut down my trees 
and sell them. You see me cart my 
furniture away and sell it. You know 
that I whittled my land up and sold 
away more than half of it. You knew 
that I was covered up with mortgages. 
And you never offered me nothing!” 

“I give you the best advice in the 
world.” 

“Hell is paved with good advice. 
You would of seen me go down the 
whole way before you so much as of- 
fered me a penny!” 

“I had my own business. How was 
I to stay awake at night wondering 
how you might be getting on?” 

“Well, you didn’t do it. And now, 
Andy, you come here whining to me, 
and you ask me to call off Peter and 
talk to him rough because he sees fit 
to talk sweet to Ruth McNair!” 

“She’s engaged to his cousin. It’s 
criminal for him to start flirting with 
a girl like that, him with his fine edu- 
cation and his soft way of yarning 
about things!” 

“Curse it!“ shouted Ross Hale. 
“You had your choice. You raised 
your son among the cattle, and I raised 
mine among the men. Now let your 
bull of a son try his own way with 
Ruth McNair. I tell you that I think 
you're right! I think that Peter is 
going to go after Ruth. I think that 
he’s going to get her, too. I hope he 
does! And when he has the McNair 


millions, I’m ‘gunna ride up to your 


house, Andy, and laugh at you. . I’m 
gunna laugh so loud that it will start 
the dogs barking.” 

Ross Hale was a reasonably hot- 
tempered man, but he was much more 
gentle than Andy. Yet there was this 
difference between them—while Ross 
had to break out with every thought 


that entered his mind, Andy ruled 
himself with a hand of iron and kept 
his tongue strictly in order. 

He ruled himself now, but his face 
turned white with passion as he listened 
to the outburst of his brother. 

“Very well!” said Andy. I've 
heard what you've got to say to-day. 
I’m going away and wait for what you 
may have to say to-morrow!” 

And he was as good as his word. 

He got his horse. Ruth McNair 
was brought from the shed and Char- 
lie called in from the pasture. The 
party went away, leaving Ross Hale 
wildly excited, filled with vague re- 
grets that he had talked so freely. But 
he said not a word to Peter. He felt, 
somehow, that the less he talked with 
Peter on this point, the better it would 
be for both of them. 

Here was a point on which Andy 
Hale was hardly so discreet, for he 
felt that the very ground had been 
shaken beneath his feet, To be sure, 
he had accomplished some good things 
and some big things in his life. No 
one could say that his ranch was not 
a triumph. At the same time, all that 
he had ever done in the past was as 
nothing compared with this grand op- 
portunity to strike gold by marrying 
his son to Ruth McNair. And if that 
chance were lost—it would be like 
death itself to Andy Hale. 

As the girl, handsome, smiling 
Charlie, and he rode over the hills to- 
ward the McNair house, and saw the 
rolling acres of the rich rancher, all 
dotted over with little colored spots 
where the cattle were feeding, it 
seemed to him that this was a veritable 
kingdom. Surely there was no youth 
in the range more worthy of this in- 
heritance. 

Who should dare to take the king- 
dom away from him? Peter, the crip- 
ple? Let Peter guard his head well 
before he contemplated such an at- 
tempt!

Peter was in the blacksmith shop. 
Indeed, he spent more time there than 


in any other portion of the ranch 


which he was revamping so swiftly and 
so successfully, He had always had a 
love for tools. On the ranch, in the 
early days, he had spent much: time in 
rough carpentering and in watching at 
the blacksmith. shops of Sumnertown. 
There was always something by. way of 
information to be picked up by a hun- 
gry eye. There was always something 
to be learned by a few adroit ques- 
tions, here and there. Above all, there 
was the love of experimentation which 
never left him. 

Then, when he went away to beau- 
tiful Huntley School, he had found 
there a well-developed manual-training 
department, for the head master be- 
lieved in such things as handicraft for 
boys! In that department of the school 
Peter had chiefly reveled. If he won 
happy moments on the athletic fields 
of the school, he won glorious hours 
of contentment at the turning lathe and, 
above all, at the forge. 

Now everything was equipped on the 


ranch to suit his crippled condition. 


In the smithy he could ‘lift himself 
easily to the swinging seat which stood 
high up and between the forge and the 


.. fire, with the handle of the bellows 
wheel near and all the clustering tools 


in reaching distance, while the temper- 
ing tub was where he could dip the 
blackening iron and pluck it out again

with a long-handled pincers. He al- 
ready had made himself more at home 
in the blacksmith shop, therefore, than 
in the rest of the ranch. 

On the day following the historic 
visit of Charlie and Ruth McNair, 
Peter was propped up, swinging his 
own peculiar sledge. Although he 
used only one hand for its management, 
he had the hammer made with a full 
twelve-pound weight in its head, and 
with a shortened handle, thick enough 
to fill his ample grasp. Hammer 
strokes are not delivered by brute force 
alone, and those who looked in to see 
Peter at his work were always amazed 
by the ease with which he wielded this 
ponderous tool. The vast shoulders 
and the long, powerful arms were not 
a sufficient explanation. Something 
more was needed, and that was the 
thythmic grace with which the work 
was done. 

He was molding a great bar of iron, 
as thick as his wrist. His face was 
blackened with soot and reddened with 
the heat of the fire. The ringing ham- 
mer strokes echoed out of the little 
shop, passing in clangorous waves far

over the corral and across the road,

You may be sure that Peter guessed

dropped the hammer and, jerking out 
a Colt, whirled around in the spinning 
seat in time to cover with his gun the 
portly form of Mr. Mike Jarvin, as 
that gentleman pushed open the little 
side door of the shop!

WILD ANIMALS OF NORTH AMERICA 
The Mongoose 
WHEN a mine shaft has been closed for a while rattlesnakes often move in 


and take possession, 
—hbefore operation is again resumed. 


Here they breed and multiply—sometimes for years 
In such instances the owner would find 


himself. in serious trouble but for the mongoose. 
This little animal, a native of India, is about the size of a small polecat, 


or a trifle larger than the ordinary ferret. 
color, with a narrow head and long, bushy tail. 
In a few days the rattlesnakes are a non-existing 


mine shaft, blocked in, and left. 


It is grizzly-brown and black in 
The mongoose is put into the 


menace—for the mongoose kills these deadly reptiles almost as quickly as our 
common ferret disposes of a rat. This is due to its quick sight and action 
rather than to its immunity to poison, as is generally supposed. The little 
animal always catches the poisonous snake directly back of its deadly, diamond- 
shaped head with its strong tusks and powerful jaws, thus breaking the rattler’s 
spine. The mongoose, therefore—it seems needless to remark—is highly thought 
of in those parts ef the West where rattlesnakes are common. 


Back Home in Arkansas 


Robert Ormond Case 


Author of “King of the Condon Range,” etc. 


WILIGHT had fallen 
the vast McTavish hold- 
ings. In the gloom of the 
shadowed veranda of the 
bunk house the cowhands 
sprawled in more or less comfortable 
postures on the battle-scarred bench, or 
leaned against the weather-beaten pil- 
lars, Cigarettes glowed redly through 
the gloom, and thin spirals of smoke 
rose dimly toward the glittering stars. 

The guardirr/ s of the McTavish 
brand were, for the most part, a hard- 
bitten, hard-riding, and reckless. crew. 
They were lean, tanned youths, cold of 
eye and square of jaw, their physical 
and mental equipment consisting mainly 
of rawhide and recklessness, 

By startling contrast with these sea- 
soned products of the cattle country, was 
Sam “Dynamite” Smith, latest and most 
youthful addition to the McTavish pay 


upon the home ranch of. 


roll.. He was a vast youth of some 
nineteen or twenty years, bulging and 
bulbous in all dimensions, plump and 
rosy of cheek, thick limbed and deep 
chested. His size alone would have 
made him ‘conspicuous in a harsh en- 
vironment hostile alike to superfluities 
of flesh or manner; but Sam had other 
characteristics as refreshing as they 
were unique. 

His progress thus far in his ambition 
to become a cow hand had been rapid 
and spectacular, This was attested by 
the fact that, despite a colossal igno- 
rance of the cattle country, he had suc- 
ceeded in attaching himself, in an in- 
credibly short space of time, to an out- 
fit that was admittedly the most reck- 
less and expert aggregation-of punchers 


and wranglers in a vast domain; and by 


the further and more amazing fact that 
he had remained on the pay roll. This 
had been due partly to his ability to 


54 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


learn quickly the fundamentals of range 
routine, partly to his indomitable cour- 
age and certain explosive potentialities, 
very thoroughly concealed beneath his 
huge exterior, but mostly to sheer, un- 
adulterated good luck. 

His education, furthermore, had pro- 


ceeded mainly in the realm of physical 


action. In the heartbreaking work of 
the range, he had taken his place among 
the iron-muscled crew, having been ac- 
cepted as one of them. In action of any 
description, in the parlance of the cattle 
country, he was invariably a crowd. 
This was conceded him unquestionably 
and without qualification. But in mo- 
ments of relaxation, the men with whom 
he worked on the range were still, to 
him, possessed of baffling and puzzling 
personalities. 

He had not yet solved the psychology 
of these reckless riders of the high 
places—men who would face death with 
a grin and weep over the plight of some 
purely fictitious character in a doleful 
cowboy song; who would cheerfully 
spend eighteen hours in the saddle per- 
forming prodigies of labor in the teeth 
of a howling blizzard, and later, beneath 
a smiling sun, protest bitterly and pro- 
fanely at the prospect of sinking a half- 
dozen post holes along the line fence; 
who would merely smile at maledictions 
heaped upon them. by a coworker and 
dive for their guns at the twitch of a 
stranger’s eyebrow. From observation, 
he knew that any member of the Mc- 
Tavish crew would willingly sign over 
his pay check and mortgage his saddle 
horse if one of his fellows required it; 
yet he had seen the entire crew stage a 
terrific imbroglio over the ownership of 
a thin dime in a crap game. More than 
once, when many of this reckless fra- 
ternity from other ranches had fore- 
gathered in Condon, he had listened, 
with muscles. tense, to loud and 
ferocious arguments from which noth- 
ing had resulted; he had disregarded 
certain softly worded and colorless ex- 


changes that had just preceded gun 
play. 

These things intrigued and baffled 
Sam, As he sat on the outer fringe of 
the group, hunched like a great toad 
on the lower step, he took no part in 
the lazy conversation. He listened and 
pondered, striving to analyze the mo- 
tives and point of view that lay behind 
the casual remarks bandied back and 
forth in the deepening gloom. His si- 
lence was not the result of bashfulness 
or timidity. His self-confidence was as 
vast as his own bulky person. But cer- 
tain incidents of the past, mortifying to 
him, although undoubtedly humorous to 
the crew, had taught him that to inject 
himself too abruptly into the discussion 
often led into conversational channels 
that were puzzling and obscure. 

The crew, on their part, despite their 
seeming casualness, were acutely con- 
scious of Sam’s presence. Sprawled in 
the gloom, apparently oblivious of his 
existence, as they talked of idle. and 
trivial subjects, their mental eyes, in a 
manner of speaking, were fixed upon 
him. To them, the colossal youth was 
a source of never-ending surprise and 
enjoyment. Long since, they had con- 
ceded him equality in the realm of 
physical action. Had it not been for 
his. undoubted courage, energy, and will- 
ingness to assume more than his share 
of the duties of the range, his blunt, 
hearty manner and his grinning self- 
confidence, would have been obnoxious 
in the extreme. Many things can be for- 
given a man whose heart is sound. But 
their secret admiratior. nd respect for 
the explosive qualities so successfully 
concealed beneath his ponderous ex- 
terior did not prevent them from mak- 
ing him a target for their ruthless and 
sardonic witticisms. The point of view 
of the vast and rosy-cheeked youth, his 
willingness to-accept all that was told 
him at its face value until proved other- 
wise, appealed to them as the pinnacle 
of excruciating humor. 


BACK HOME IN ARKANSAS 


Nor was their enthusiasm for badger- 
ing Sam dampened by the memory of 
certain incidents of the past when, due 
to the very sincerity of the colossal 
youth, their humorous plans had re-

As they talked aimlessly in the gloom,

veritable barrage of bland misinforma- -

The lazy conversation on the veranda 
ceased suddenly as an unexpected and 
diverting altercation broke out at the 
cook house some hundred feet distant in 
the shadow of the cottonwoods.- The 
quiet of the night was shattered by a 
sharp blast of profanity. Simulta- 
neously, the door of the cook house 
burst open and through this rectangle of 
light, the figure of old “Nevada” 
plunged forth, his hat clutched in his 
hand. Despite his years and his al- 
leged crippled condition due to rheu- 
matism, Nevada’s exit was startlingly 
abrupt. 

Then the doorway framed the raging 
figure of “Panhandle,” venomous cook 
of the McTavish outfit. That worthy 
halted on the threshold and shook his 
hairy fist at the darkness that enveloped 
his quarry. 

“Come back here,” he invited loudly 
and with considerable spirit. “Just stick 
your nose inside this kitchen again and 
watch me carve off a yard of it. And 
that goes for all you buzzards, every 
man of you. For. every hombre that 
comes into this shack till mealtime, 
there's going to be a murder. Yeah, 
and if any of you mangy polecats think 
I don’t mean it, just waltz in here, and 
you'll get to know right pronto what 
mincemeat thinks about.”

among the barren pinnacles whence his

siveness and utter brutality. His eyes,

himself. Yet Panhandle’s manner told

Thus the presence of that sinister

Notwithstanding ‘his puzzled embar-

He knew himself to be a master artist 
of the harmonica. 

In the hands of the novice, the notes 
of the harmonica are full, rounded, 
and mechanical. Even the faultless 
technique of the more. skillful, while 
pleasing at the time, serves but to em- 
phasize the limits of a reed instrument 
whose notes are monotonous in their 
very perfection. Sam's size had en- 
abled him to scale heights hitherto un- 
explored. His great hands covered the 
bell-shaped openings at the base of the 
double reeds, His big mouth covered a 
third of the keys. These mechanical 
aids to an unerring mygical instinct 
opened up for him a limitless field of 
expression. 

There was a touch of the ludicrous in 
his appearance as he began to play. His 
smooth cheeks puffed with the effort; 
his great shoulders shook. One tawny 
lock of hair hung lew over keen blue 
eyes that were fixed upon the cook.

Beneath the tan of his harsh features,

“This business puts me in a jack

But every once in a while when I got

would come to grief on my account. 
Except me—and I don’t make a prac-

“P. S. Write to your ma once in a while.” 


Panhandle rose abruptly and strode to 
the window. For a long moment, he 
stood looking out into the night. “It’s 
funny;” he muttered, turning back. “I 
thought I heard a noise outside. Do 


you suppose that blasted pack rat is 


prowling around?” 


Later, as Sam stumbled toward the 
bunk house in the gloom, his thoughts 
were in a turmoil. He felt that he had 
looked upon a phase of life that was 
new to him, yet old as time. It was odd 
that he should be enveloped with Pan- 
handle’s own somber mood. Home and 
mother? Without shame, he realized 
that tears of remorse were close to the 
surface. He must write home imme- 
diately. He was aghast at the length 
of time that had elapsed since he had 
written home. 

As for Panhandle himself, the last 
words of that hard-bitten old-timer 
were evidence that he had emerged phil-

osophically from his black moment. 
“You’ve done me a lot of. good, son,”

to home for twenty years. That’s 
longer than lots of folks can hold their 
boys. Rivers run downhill, don’t they? 
The wind blows continuous up in the 
high country, huh? Yeah, and. boys 
leave home. Them’s natural laws. 
Life’s a funny proposition. Ain’t it the

them bull knots. I’ve got to split some 
kindling if them swivel-jointed buck- 
aroos is to eat any ham and eggs in the 
morning.” 

Sam was surprised to find the bunk 
house still lighted. None of the cow 
hands, save Nevada, had retired. That

“Me, I ain’t got any folks.”

gaged in writing letters. Several were thoughts in written form. 


A MINING BOOM IN LEADFIELD 


PRIMITIVE mining camp conditions are to be found in the new settlement 

of Leadfield, in Death Valley. In the early days of the camp, water was 
sold for ten cents a gallon, and none was available for bathing purposes. Those 
desiring to bathe went to Beatty, Nevada, twenty-two miles away, or to the hot 
springs resort in the Amargoso Valley, formerly used by the Piute and Shoshone 
Indians for medicinal purposes. 

Conditions resembling those of the days of 49 in California and of 98 in 
the Yukon are reported in Leadfield. Freight is hauled from Beatty at a cost 
of $17.50 a ton. A small can of condensed milk costs twenty cents; canned 
peaches are sixty cents; candles, two for a quarter; bread, twenty cents a loaf; 
bacon, sixty cents a pound, and flour, nine dollars a half barrel. 

Bootleg whisky is said to be plentiful with little or no restriction placed 
on its sale to white men, although there is a ban on supplying it to Indians. 
Two concert halls in rough shacks and seven saloons are doing a flourishing 
business. Stud and draw poker and blackjack are the favorite games of chance. 
The settlement comprises board shacks, which are being thrown up as rapidly as 
possible. They are usually occupied as soon as they are completed. The primitive 
life of the Western frontier seems to have come te its ewn again in the new 
mining camp of Leadfield.

E small flat-bottom river 
boat wallowed dangerously 
“il as it rushed between the 
green, dripping walls of 

the gorge. Leary's face 
== paled beneath the heavy 
coating of tan, but he remained cool. 
With skillful maneuvering he passed the 
whirlpools, then the craft shot into the 
sunlight—and white water. The stream 
was lower than Leary had expected, un- 
known boulders appeared here and 
there, wet and black, waiting fangs ready 
to slash at anything the river might bring 
them. In the bow a malemute dog 
crouched. Out of his element, the dog 
was uneasy, yet such was his faith in 
the man that he did not tremble nor 
whimper. “Down! Buck. Way down!” 
The dog flattened himself obediently. 
The man’s face grew paler, and with a 
stifled cry of despair he jerked on the 
starboard oar. The next instant the 
boat lifted and dropped. Briefly it held 
while the boulder gnawed a hole in the 


shattered bottom, then it slipped off and 
crashed again and again as it was whirled 
down stream. 

Leary had acted quickly during the 
short seconds the boat had remained on 
the rock. Using his oar as a pole vaulter 
handles a pole, Leary had driven the 
blade into a small crack, vaulted a small 
channel through which the water raced 
furiously, and plunged into the back- 
water. The struggle all but exhausted 
his strength, but in the end he reached 
the bank. For a long time he lay gasp- 
ing for breath, then he took stock of his 
chances of getting out of the country. 
His food was gone, so were rifle, blan- 
kets, and his matches except a few in 
the waterproof box he always carried 
in his pocket. His assets consisted of 
a strong body, a knife, and the matches, 
In this country, the gold he had come 
for, had found, and had now lost, was 
of secondary importance to food. He 
cursed the rapids until his fury was 
spent, then began to make his way down-

stream. First he sought dry wood that. 
he might build a fire and dry out any- 
thing the stream might have hurled to 
shore. Buck of course must.be drowned. 
Even as fierce and powerful a dog as the 
malemute had little chance in such cruel 
waters. No living thing could survive 
the crushing impact against the boul- 
ders. A mere touch had splintered his 
stout craft, a series of lighter touches 
had scattered it over the stream. 

As he made his way along the rough 
shore he found bits of his outfit lodged 
against rocks or swirling about in the 
eddies. Once he plunged in to his neck 
and reclaimed a torn blanket. It was a 
well-worn Hudson's Bay four point; 
once dried out it would keep him warm 
when he slept. At a bend in the river 
he found some dry wood. Here he built 
a fire, stripped, and dried his things. It 
was dark by the time he again dressed. 
The blanket was damp, so he kept warm 
by sleeping between the fire and a log. 
He was hungry, but not as hungry as he 
would be in days to come. 

Dawn brought wakefulness, the first 
sharp pangs of hunger, and hope which 
is one of the blessings of a new day. 
Leary tightened his belt and examined 
the blanket. It was dry. He rolled it up 


tightly, shouldered it, and started down-- 


stream. 

A mile further on he stopped and 
drew his knife. Just ahead some animal 
was gulping down food. The man re- 
solved to possess the food. The animal 
could either slink off or fight it out. 
Keeping down wind Leary crawled for- 
ward, then his expression changed from 
caution to delight. “Buck!” he shouted, 
“Buck, old sport! Darn your skin, you 
made it!” 

The dog looked up, then backed away 
as Leary rushed toward him. A side of 
bacon cast ashore by the stream lay 
between them. The dog had eaten a por- 
tion of one corner... That's my food,” 
the man said sharply, “mine!” Then he 
took the knife and trimmed off the edges

where the dog had chewed. He trimmed 
very, close and such. scraps as fell off

and até that also. Then he set a course 
directly from the river. There was a 
chance in a hundred that he might beat 
starvation to a trading post the other 
side of the divide. 

He did not eat again that day, but the 
following morning he broiled another 
strip for himself and gave the dog a piece 
of the rind. Throughout the day he 
plodded steadily, saying nothing, keep- 
ing to his course. Towards night he 
stumbled through a swamp and for the 
first time realized that his strength was 
going rapidly. His legs were like lead, 
each step was an effort, and the muck 
of the swamp added pounds to the 
weight he carried. He ate a little bacon 
that night but did not give the dog any. 
And the following morning he ate again. 
The dog crouched at his feet, his eyes on 
the man’s face, waiting. The man 
turned away. “It’s life or death,” he 
muttered; “it’s my food! I’ve got to 
get through, Buck. You—you’re a— 
dog.” Almost defensively he added, 
“And sometimes up here men have eaten 
the tops of their shoes, their belts, and 
their—dogs.” 

When Leary set off once more Buck 
obediently followed. The day was much 
the same as other days—endless miles of 
rolling country and a ridge of mountains 
notching the sky. The vastness of the 
plain, the height of the peaks, seemed to 
remind the man of the futility of his 
efforts. Leary traveled all day without 
seeming to lessen the distance. That 
night he rested, and the following morn- 
ing he again cut a strip of bacon and 
broiled. it. Again the dog watched him, 
but this time he begged for a portion, 
hunching himself forward and with 
grotesque little mannerisms making his 
plea. Leary turned his back, as befare.

He was a man, and therefore should 
survive if possible. A dog did not mat- 
ter, except to the dog. To him life was 
as sweet as to the man. Vet the silent 
plea left its mark. During the day when 
the dog had set forth in futile pursuit 
of a caribou the man turned sharply and 
forded a swift stream. “He can’t fol- 
low,” Leary muttered, “and that'll make 
it easier for me.” 

Each day thereafter the man made 
some progress. He was always hungry 
but the fine condition of his body after 
a summer of hard work in the open, 
was carrying him along when a weaker 
man must have fallen on the short ration. 
Occasionally he tried to bring down a 
rabbit with a crude bow and arrow he 
had made. But the arrows failed to go 
straight, and in the end he gave it up. 
As his food supply grew less, he forced 
himself more and more. If he could but 
just gain the summit and start down the 
glacier he might encounter some other 
prospector or perhaps sight a camp fire 
in the vast country beyond. 

When he reached the base of the 
mountains and began the upward climb 
he found himself growing dizzy, his 
mind did not respond to his efforts to 
reason. He moved more by instinct than 
by planned direction, following a rough 
water course that began at a glacier. 
The water was white and dirty from the 
mud and sand it carried; there were no 
fish in the stream. Leary drank from 
the frequent springs feeding the stream 
on either side. Twice he encountered 
old camps, and he searched for bits of 
scraps that might remain. 

When he reached the summit the wind 
whipping through the peaks knocked 
him down. For a mile he crawled over 
the ice, then with a glad cry began the 
descent, following a glacial arm extend- 
ing from the main glacier to the pass 
through which he had just made his way. 
Stupidly he peered -down on the empire 
below. It was as vast as the country he 
had left behind and as barren of habi- 


tation. There was game, of course, but 
he could not catch it barehanded. He 
believed now that he would die. He 
struggled to his feet and began the grad- 
ual descent. The last of his food was 
gone. His situation was the same as 
Buck’s—both were without food. “Poor 
old Buck,” he muttered, “he was too 
slow to catch a rabbit. Buck tried it 
again and again, and he always failed.” 

There were no more heartbreaking 
climbs to block his way, but neither did 
he have the strength that had carried 
him to the summit. Progress from this 
time consisted of falling, rising, crawl- 
ing. Again and again he was tempted 
to discard the weight of his blanket, but 
this he needed at night. So he kept the 
four point on his back when its twelve- 
pound weight seemed more like a full 
pack. f 

Each night he built a fire and heated 
water in an empty tomato can that 
he had found. Hot water did much to 
drive the chill, now always with him, 
from his body. He managed to sleep 
warm until midnight, then with his re- 
sistance at the lowest, he shivered until 


‘morning. The desire to abandon the 


blanket and be on his way had left him 
completely. He wanted to lie there and 
wait for the end. But when the sun 
came its warmth seemed to insist that 
he live another day. He warmed his 
hands, drank more hot water, and con- 
tinued his erratic course. He could not 
see the end of the glacier, but he knew 
it was miles ahead. It ended in a great 
stream, and the first cabin was many 
miles downstream. 

When the day came that he did not 
want to build a fire, Leary knew it must 
be next to the last. He found himself 
kneeling on the ice, stupidly folding and 
unfolding his blanket, seeking some 
means of access to its warmth. He shook 
his head, arranged it, then crawled in. 
Even a system at the lowest ebb will 
respond somewhat to a night’s rest. 
When the sun came out he looked at the

red disk for a long time, then by degrees

with my knife, I can kill the living one

can rest in my blanket and make it.” 

Then it was he discarded the blanket. 
If he won out he could return for the 
blanket, if he lost there would be no

Leary became somewhat of an, animal 
himself as he approached the scene of 
the struggle. The first thing he saw 
was the rib bene of a large animal. It 
had been gnawed and actually broken by

heavy. Just ahead he heard snarling. 
With drawn knife he crawled closer. 
Yes, they were fighting; two wolves, or 
was one a dog? It mattered not, he 
would wait like a vulture until the two 
had wasted their strength on each other, 
then he would strike. He laughed wildly, 
and there was a note in the laughter that 
silenced him by its very lack of mirth. 
I'm going nutty,” he chuckled, “plumb 
nutty.” He rubbed his bearded face 
and sebbed. “No flesh, just whiskers 
and cheek and jaw bones.”

less, the killing had been easy enough. 
Now another was claiming the kill. 
“That big fellow defending his kill is

like Buck. Yes, he looks like Buck, but 
Buck is dead. Poor Buck. I took his 
food from him. That side of bacon was 
his by rights; he found it. He gave it 
to me because I was a man, and he was 
a dog, and he was taught to obey me. 
I took his food, but I couldn’t eat it with 
him looking at me that way. So I left 
him.” 

The incident seemed to disturb his dis- 
torted mind. He muttered something 
about taking Buck’s bacon several times. 
The animals were circling warily, each 


‘seeking an opportunity to slash the other. 


The fangs of each were keen and power- 
ful. He could see the jaws of each drip- 
ping. “A lot like Buck,” he muttered, 
“a lot!” The dog turned, and Leary 
cried out in joy. The dog’s shoulder 
was worn on the left side by harness. 
It was Buck. Buck!“ he screamed. 
At him Buck. Get him, boy!“ 

But it was a different Buck. He 
either did not hear or else he ignored 
the man’s cry. He was a creature of 
the wild, and he had starved a period 
as he followed the homing instinct; then 
he had become desperate and brought 
down a caribou at the cost of a slight 
wound. The taste of blood had aroused 


‘instincts long dead. He gorged himself 


at his kill again and again, then had left 
it, confident that he could kill again

been but little food after he crossed the

had taken once before when Leary had 
gone prospecting. 

He was half starved when he had 
caught the scent of the moose. It had 
taken him a half day to maneuver it to

the point where he could bring it down 
unaided. And now a wolf, as desper- 
ately in need of food as himself, chal-
