He had fought wolf dogs too many 
times not to know their methods. There 
was a wolf strain in his own powerful 
body. He did not bite as the domestic 
dog bites. Instead his was a darting 
slash that came so quickly as to make 
escape impossible. When he missed as 
he frequently did, the click of his teeth 
was like the snapping of a steel trap. 

The sme‘! of warm blood from the 
dying moose drove the wolf to despera- 
tion. He abruptly became a thing of 
fury and charged. His jaws slashed 
Buck’s side but the grip did not hold, 
and an instant later Buck’s teeth had 
met in the other’s throat. For a long

the dog retained his hold. When the

toward his kill. Now he would gorge

Leary came closer. “Buck!” he cried, 
“Buck!” 

Buck turned and snarled. Another 
creature was crawling toward him. It 
was hungry and. wanted his kill. He

ing, Buck. I want meat.” 

The dog advanced, snarling, and the

I took your bacon. Once again it’s you 
or me. It's your meat, Buck, but like 
the wolf, I’m going to take it if I can.” 

iLeary crawled closer like a wounded 


į 


animal on three legs. The fourth limb, 
Leary’s right hand, held the knife. His 
bony knuckles stood out white against 
the drawn skin. All of his last strength 
would go into a single thrust. If he 
won, he would eat. If he lost he would

“Buck!” he pleaded, “old fellow, I’m 
hungry. Don’t you know me, Buck?” 

The malemute’s nose, clogged with 
blood from the wolf, lifted in a snarl. 
He seemed infuriated beyond restraint 
at this second challenge for his kill. The 
hair lifted on his back as he advanced 
to meet the new. danger. Almost furi- 
ously he snorted and felt once more 
the cold air of the glacier in his nostrils. 
With a rush he was on Leary. The 
knife flashed and missed its mark. 
Leary was rolled over. Buck’s hot breath 
was in his face. ‘Buck’s fangs were 
within inches of his throat, and Buck’s 
tongue licked his cheek. Buck’s fury 
had vanished with the scent of the mas- 
ter, his joy was the joy of madness. He 
licked Leary’s face, and the fear that 
had come into the sunken eyes vanished, 
and hope returned. The man’s arms 


went around the dog’s neck, the man’s 


fingers examined the dog’s wounded 
shoulder. Then Leary moved toward 
the moose. 

Once more the man took possession 
of the dog’s food, but this time master 
and dog shared alike. The dog gorged 
himself, but the man saved his share 
and ate sparingly lest his weakened 
stomach rebel. Later he returned for his 
blanket. For like the creatures of the 
wild, the man must remain close to the 
kill until strength returned.

By S. Omar Barker 


RAY hoss, black hoss, pinto, roan, 
Bronchos tough as the devil’s own. 
I’ve spent life with my legs a-straddle 
Of a buckin’ hoss and a cowboy saddle. 
Some bucks straight and some all around, 
Some snorts skyward; some paws ground. 
Some, tis said, weren’t never rade. 
But I stays on—if I don’t git throwed— 
Plumb to the end of the jamboree, 
Ridin’ ’em fair and scratchin’ ’em free! 


Ride ’em Clean : 
| 


Never a bronc that I wouldn't mount: 

Now I’m old and J ain't no-count— 

Gray hoss, black hoss, pinto, roan, 

Bronchos tough as the devil’s own. 

Ride ’em as they come, boy, never mind the weather! 
Give ’em free rein and don’t pull leather! 

Stay if yuh can, but ride ’em clean! 

That’s bronc stompin’—the kind I mean.. 

Yuh’re still winner if yuh git throwed flat, 

Ridin’ of ’em clean, boy—life’s like that!

Trail of the Stone-hat-shines 


Peter Henry Morland 


Author of “The White Cheyenne,” ete. 


Synopsis of Preceding Chapters 
DY” te being ruined at the raee track, Dick Rutherford, The Colonel, leaves Virginia to seek his 
fortune in the West, se that he may return to marry Martha Farnsworth. With him he takes 
the twelve-year-old Christy Deever, of whom he has made a confidant and bodyguard. 

They make the overland tour on horses and by boat. While on the plains they are attacked 
by an Indian whom The Colonel shoots. On the latter’s sparing the redskin’s life, the Indian, 
Sanja, becomes The Colonel’s stanch friend. i 

Sanja takes them to the Mandan village, where they are received with open arms by Ompah, 
the chief. A beautiful Indian maiden, The Quail, is intrigued by The Colonel, partieularly after 
he rescues her from a burning lodge, during a terrific storm. 

This storm was supposedly brought about by the incantations of a brave, Stone-that-shines, who 
exposes his sacred medicine bag in whieh he has some gold that he has discovered. The Colonel 
learns from him a little as to where he has discovered the precious metal, which the Indian 
values only as “medicine.” 3 

In a battle with the Sioux, there are three heroes: Stone-that-shines, Bending Feather, a dandy 
who has heretofore been held in contempt, and The Colonel. The latter exploits his glory by finding 
more about the location of the white mountain” Where Stone-that-shines discovered the treasure. 
Christy and the former then prepare to go out after it.

A MARRIAGE TRANSACTION.

chief, came to call on the warrior and

After that Ompah returned to his 
house and ordered ten of his selected 
best horses to be got together. And 
the whole ten, each equipped with some 
article of harness—whether a saddle or 
a bridle or some other prized posses-

Harratta lived and tethered close to the

It was the marriage price for The 
Quail, and the whole Mandan. popula- 
tion turned out to stare at this nine- 
day wonder. From one to three horses 
was the usual price; perhaps four 
would go for the pick of the girls of 
a whole tribe. If she happened to be 
the daughter of a great chief, and 
therefore brought a slice of political in- 
fluence to her husband, she might bring 
a top figure of five horses, but this was 
very rare and something te be talked 
about everywhere and for many days 
to come. You could travel a long dis- 
tamce among the tribes before you got 
to a five-horse woman. But here was a 
staggering price of tem horses, paid 
down without preliminary dickering by 
the head chief of a tribe te a mere 
warrior in the ranks of his followers. 
Such a man as Ompah could have had 
a bride for the asking, and welcome, 
from any lodge in the city. But out he 
stepped with a truly kingly generosity 
and tied ten ponies at the lodge of 
Harratta. More than that, he put some 
article of riding furniture on the back 
of each—three saddles, among other 
things! In the eyes of the Mandans 
it was divine, because gemerosity was 
always considered to raise a brave to 
the level of the superhumen. 

The Colonel and I went among the 
rest to see the show. Thes we stood 
about to see the sight of The Quail led 
in procession by her father to the 
ledge of the war elef. There were 
very few absent from the Mandan 


81 


gathering which hung around for the 
same purpose. But Harratta did not 
appear with The Quail. We heard a 
great deal of unfavorable comment. 
The Mandans thought Harratta, a 
shrewd trader, was trying to boost his 
“daughter even above this unprecedented 
figure of ten horses, and there were 
murmured exclamations of disgust.. 

“But,” said Sanja to us, “you must 
know that Harratta is not a true Man- 
dan.” 

He went on to tell us that Harratta 
was astray who had been picked up 
with his family on the prairie by a 
Mandan war party. He took them in 
and entertained them liberally, and he 
was finally invited to come to their 
city, since it was obvieus that he was 
an outcast. This had happened four 
or five years ago, and he had been with 
them ever since. Sanja did not know 
the real tribe of Harratta. The lan- 
guage which Harratta spoke when he 
was brought to the Mandans had not 
been recognized by any ef them, and 
he had soon melted himself into the 
ways of his adopted race. Such adop- 
tions, in fact, were very common on 
the plains. However, there was some 
excuse for a bit of sharp practice on 
the part of Harratta, for it was 
pointed out that he had lost many valu- 
able possessions during the fire which 
the lightning had started in his lodge. 

All the conjectures proved to be 
wrong. After a time there was a dis- 
tinct murmuring from the Indians who 
stood nearest to the new lodge in which 
Harratta was with his daughter. 
Presently the word came humming to 
us that Harratta was flogging his 
girl unmercifully. A dead hush fell 
over the crowd, and we could hear the 
strokes of the whip! 

The Colonel was thrown into an 
agony. He wanted to ge into that 
lodge and wring the neck of Harratta 
and throw his head to the crows. I 
hung onto his arm, talking as fast as 


82 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


I could, trying to persuade him that we 
knew nothing about the right and 
wrong of this thing, from the Man- 
dan viewpoint. Besides, perhaps The 
Quail deserved a little disciplining. She 
had always seemed a pretty high- 
. headed sort to me. 

“Red, black, or white,” said The 
Colonel, “a woman is a woman, and a 
brute is a brute. How can the rest of 
these fellows stand around and let this 
thing happen?” 

However, I had my way, and The 
Colonel did not interfere, though it 
cost him a lot of pain to hold his hands. 
In the meantime, the Mandans began 
to whisper. Such a thing as a girl 
refusing to marry the head war chief 
of the tribe had never been heard of 
before. Besides, she would be the first 
wife, and had a good chancé of being 
the only one which the chief would 
take—which made her conduct all the 
more mysterious. f 

After that, out burst Harratta, with 
his feathers in disarray and his face 
covered with perspiration. He dragged 
The Quail behind him and took her 
straight to the lodge where Ompah was 
sitting in the sun smoking his long pipe, 
apparently oblivious to the commotion. 
which he had raised. Harratta threw 
the girl down at the feet of the chief 
and made a brief speech at the top of 
his lungs. 

He said that he accepted the price 
which the chief paid; he was glad to 
make this honorable alliance with a 
man as great as Ompah. As for thé 
girl, she was a little refractory, but 
that did not matter. Ompah knew how 
to rule a whole tribe, and therefore he 
would know how to handle one woman. 

Then Harratta turned on his heel 
and strode off, patently washing his 
hands of the whole matter. He was 
much applauded as having played the 
part of a good father and a wise In- 
dian, who knew a good bargain when 
he came to it. In white society he 


would have been considered an un- 
speakable scoundrel. But standards, 
were a bit different on the prairies. 

In the meantime, Ompah stood up 
and raised the girl to her feet. She 
shook the dust out of her dress of 
deerskin and brushed back her long, 
dusky hair. She was the calmest per- 
son in that whole tribe! We could see 
Ompah inviting her into his lodge with 
a gesture. If she stepped across his 
threshold, by that act, she became his 
wife. Furthermore, he had the right to 
pick her up and carry her, since the 
ceremony was now left in his hands. 
Apparently he didn’t choose to do that. 
He merely stood there trying the force 
of words, and words weren’t able to 
budge this stubborn girl. She merely 
smiled at him and past him, seeming 
hardly to know that he was speaking 
to her. 

He had a big string of blue and red 
beads around his neck. That he took 
off, at last, and draped around her 
throat. Then he made a gesture which 
gave her leave to go where she pleased, 
and there was a veritable groan of as- 
tonishment from the Mandans. 

I asked Sanja if Ompah would now 
go and lead back the ten horses, but 
Sanja said that he wouldn’t unless 
Harratta took the girl back to his lodge 
as a member of his family. In the 
meantime, the price had been paid, and 
Harratta had done all that could be ex- 
pected from him to make good the 
price of the ten horses, so far as the 
girl was concerned. 

So there was a new center of inter- 
est, of course, and we saw the girl go 
back to her father’s lodge, walking 
through mutterings of disgust and 
contempt and surprise on the part of 


the braves. The women weren’t so 
soft spoken. They didn’t hesitate for 
words. The strongest language was 


the best, on an occasion like this, it 
seemed, and they used talk that would 
have made tramps blush like girls. 


TRAIL OF THE STONE-THAT-SHINES 83 


Among other things which were their 
politest remarks, they told her that she 
was a disgrace to her mother and to 
her father, that she was fit to be 
flogged from the town and turned loose 
in the prairie, that it would be wise to 
tie her to a stake and set a fire under 
her. 

She marched on through them as if 
they didn’t exist, and her calmness in- 
furiated them all the more, until one 
long-armed hag grabbed her by the 
long hair and jerked her backward. 

It was nearly the last act of that hag 
on this earth, I assure you. I have 
seen lots of fast handwork, but noth- 
ing with a snakier speed than the man- 
ner in which The Quail drew a knife 
and made a pass at the other woman. 
There was a scream and a backward 
tumble; then The Quail walked on se- 
renely until she came to us. Here she 
paused and gave The Colonel a smile 
that was as plain as a three-inch head- 
line printed in red. It said: 

“Tf you approve of what I’ve done, 
I don’t care a rap for the opinions of 
the rest of this gang!“ 

Then she went on, leaving The Colo- 
nel fairly done up and leaning on my 
shoulder, gasping: “What the devil 
does it mean, Christy?“ 

“It means murder,” said I, “unless 
we get ourselves out of this town dou- 
ble quick. I happened to see the face 
of Ompah when he saw the girl pause 
here by us. And Ompah is thinking up 
a worse storm than Stone-that-shines 
ever dreamed of!” 

The Quail finally reached the en- 
trance to her father’s new lodge, but 
old Harratta stood at the entrance with 
his pipe in his hand, drawing long 
puffs and blowing them at the blue of 
the sunny sky. There wasn’t any doubt 
as to where The Quail got her stony 
facial expression. There was her ex- 
emplar standing in front of her. For 
a moment they just stared at each 
other. Finally Harratta said: 


“Squaw, where is your work? There 
is none for you in this house. All the 
room in it is filled!” 


CHAPTER XXIV. 
GIFTS. 


THERE the thing was, tied in a knot 
which couldn't be solved, so far 
as I could see. The Quail no longer 
belonged to her father. She would not 
go to the man who had the legal right 
upon her. There was no tepee in the 
village that was open to her. What 
would happen now? 
I looked at The Colonel. He had 
closed his eyes like a man in intense 


-pain, and his forehead was wrinkled 


with suffering. Then he said to me: 

“What is she doing, Christy? I 
can’t watch her. Confound her, I can’t 
make myself see what she’s doing!” 

“She’s not coming back here,” I told 
him. She's going off through the 
town, as calm as you please.” 

“And where will she go?” The Colo- 
nel groaned. 

I gave him a quick glance and a 
mighty anxious one. “Where she goes 
doesn’t matter to us,” I told him. “I 
hope that we don’t have to take the 
burden of responsibility for her!” 

“Oh, Christy,“ said he, you have a 
heart of stone! A heart of stone!” 

“T hope that it’s no stonier than 
yours,” I told him. “Because if yours 
should be too soft, now, it would just 
about mean the finish of the pair of us. 
Be hard-hearted, Colonel, if you want 
to wear your scalp back to good old 
Virginia.” 

“Right,” said he. “As usual, you 
are right, and I’m a blithering idiot. 
Besides, I have some one else to think 
about! I have some one else to think 
about!” 

I was glad that he had not made up 
his mind to be foolish, but, all the 
same, I didn’t like the way that he had 
to argue with himself. I would have 


34 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


been gladder to see him come to the 
same conclusion by instinct. But I 
tried to drop the thing out of my head 
and cross no bridges whatever until I 
came to them. Well, I hurried on the 
preparations for our departure as fast 
as I could. 

“We have to give things,” said The 
Colonel to me. “That leather coat of 
mine, and your old one—we could give 
those away. And then—I think that 
we might part with one of our guns, 
eh? Besides 5 

Colonel,“ said I, you leave it all 
to me!” 

J was frightened to death, hearing 
him carry on like this. Why, he would 
have had to have a whole cartful of 
stuff, if he started out making pres- 
ents on a scale like this. I had another 
plan. We had a few beads, which I 
had stuffed into the packs. before we 
left Virginia. In addition, we had 
some other knickknacks which I had 
dropped into a junk bag from time to 
time—broken buttons, needles, bits of 
thread, and a pocketknife with one of 
the blades snapped off short—in short, 
everything which The Colonel would 
have thrown away. I thought these 
things would do very nicely for the 
Mandans, if only I could persuade The 
Colonel to give them! 

Suddenly inspired, I took some bits 
of paper’ and made a flat envelope for 
each of those little gifts. Then I laid 
them all out for The Colonel, with the 
name of each man written on the up- 
per right-hand corner, all very regular 
and fine. I said: 

“You don’t have to worry. I’ve got 
an appropriate gift for every one of 
those red beggars. All that you have 
to do is to make a speech with every 
gift that you give, so as to make those 
fellows think that you are extremely 
fond of them, and that, nothing but 
your great love for them could make 
you part with such valuable things!” 

I didn’t tell him what the items were, 


and he didn’t ask me. He only won- 
dered what I could have got together 
that was so small. 

“And I don’t miss a single thing, 
Christy,” said he. 

“Tve given them more than they’re 
worth,” said I. “Besides, haven’t we 
fought for those scoundrels?” 

“Here’s the packet for Four Buck 
Elk,” said The Colonel. “It seems a 
bit slim for such an important fellow 
as he is!” 

It was a bit of broken brass button. 
But what difference did that make? It 
could be sewed on a coat or, since it 
was bright and shiny, it would do to 
make the centerpiece of a necklace, 
There was no end to the possibilities 
of that button for an Indian. 

“I explained that the gift was just 
what would take Four Buck’s eye, and 
while I was about it The Colonel took 
his péncil and knocked off a free-hand 
rapid sketch of that warrior. He got 
the main features—the long ears with 
the earrings that pulled them down, the 
low, flat forehead, and bulging jaws. 
He went on to the other envelopes and 
did the same for each; he put them in 
profile, catching the resemblances with 
a few sweeping cuts of his pencil. 

He said that it would keep him from 
making any mistakes when he handed 
out my gifts. But when he got down 
the list to Sanjakakokah he hesitated, 
saying that he could hardly let old 
Sanja off with only a little, light thing 
such as this envelope, and so he laid 
out a single-barreled pistol for that 
brave. 

When we were all ready, we started 
making our tour of the camp, and the 
first place that we went to, of course, 
was to the head chief, Ompah. He was 
very gracious, and he said that he 
couldn’t take anything from our hands, 
because we had done a great deal for 
the Mandans and helped them in their 
battle “with the guns that do not miss, 
even when very young warriors use

them,” said Ompah, and waved his

the sun does not throw the shadow that

There was a good deal of nose on

He came out again on the run, pretty

the next slip of paper containing the 
sketch of Flying Dust himself. That 
husky young brave nearly fainted 
when he saw his ugly, smashed-in face

heart. He was the happiest man in the 
world! He told The Colonel that he 
was his father and that this medicine 
would keep him, Flying Dust, safe in 
battle, so long as the spears of the en- 
emy could not come at this image of 
him! 

Yes, sir, that was the sort of medi- 
cine that was tied onto this poor free- 
hand drawing by the Mandans! As 
soon as the idea was suggested, it never 
occurred to any one to doubt its effi- 
cacy. From that instant on, this was 
believed to be the gospel truth! 

The Colonel started to explain, but I

- stamped on his foot with my heel. And 

even that was hardly enough to keep 
him quiet. Honesty is a vice with some 
men, and The Colonel was one of 
them! 

However, we made one sad heart. 
When Sanja came to his turn and got 
nothing but a pistol—which was better 
than any other in the tribe’s possession 
and worth at least four or five good 
horses, or a couple of hard-working 
Mandan wives—he couldn’t contain his 
disappointment. I whispered to The 


Colonel, and to make things right he 


spent half an hour with a pot of red 
paint and a brush making a life-sized 
picture of Sanja in full war regalia. 
It had a profile that looked like Sanja 
or Sanja’s cousin, perhaps. It was big, 
it was red, and it was medicine. 

When the sun set that day Sanja was 
the happiest and the most envied man in 
the prairies—or in the world, for all 
that I know. 


CHAPTER XXV. 
ON THE WAY. 


You may gather from this that when 

we departed from the Mandans we 
were fairly popular. I mean, The 
Colonel was popular, and since I went 
along with him I was not hated. Only, 
as we rode out of the town, leading our 
pack horses along with us, I passed Two 
Big Buffalo. He laid his hand covertly 
and significantly upon the handle of his 
knife, as much as to say that some time 
he would get at me and have our lit- 
tle argument out. However, I didn’t 
worry about such a small thing as that, 
for the moment. 

The Colonel had dodged a whole 
avalanche of return gifts by swearing 
that we intended to return to the Man- 
dan town, and that then we would be 
delighted to take whatever the kindness 
of the Indians suggested: Then he 
made me ride along with him, though I 
begged him to at least accept two or 


three of the best-looking horses be- 
cause horses were always handy on the 
prairies—you couldn’t have too many of 
them. 

He wouldn’t listen to reason, and as 
soon as we got out of earshot of the 
Mandans, who escorted us the first few 
miles out of the city and heaped our 
horses with meat, raw and dried, I told 
The Colonel what was in my mind. 

He was very bland. “Why, Christy,” 

said he, “should we take the possessions 
of those poor people?” 
Sir,“ said I, “there are twenty men 
in that town who could sell their goods 
—white man’s money—for twenty 
times as much as you could get for 
yours.” 

He bit his lip. “Darn it, Christy, 
don’t bear down on me so hard, will 
you? Let up a bit, old man!” 

He went on: “We were the guests 
of these people. You can’t spend a 
week-end at a house and then let the 
host give you the brass knocker off 
his front door as you go away, and his 
pet riding horse as well!” 

“Sir,” said I, “you’ve filled that town 
with medicine so big that they'll never 
stop blessing you!” 

“Those sketches? Not bad things, in 
their own way,” said The Colonel. 
“Very odd, Christy, how savages like 
that have an eye for art! Sort of an 
instinct for it. They recognized the 
merit in those sketches. There seemed 
to be something in my pencil when I 
was working. Inspiration, I'd call it! 
That thing of Sanja—not bad, I’d say! 
Good thing, of its kind 

That was The Colonel's weak point, 
you understand. He was the most mod- 
est man in the world, until he got to 
talking about his art. Then he was apt 
to be a bit foolish. 

“Yes,” said I, “it’s good as Indian 
paintings go. Compared with the rest 
of the paintings in that town, yours are 


first rate. Even the Mandans could see 


that.”

a thousand dollars’ worth of horses, be- 
sides the trimmings!” 

“Well,” said he, defending himself,

“Need them to carry the gold that

pleased him. didn’t please me, and he 
couldn’t breathe in air that was plenty 
good enough for me. That was all that 
there was to it. 

We made a good long march on that 
first day, cutting off to the west, in the 
direction which, as nearly as we could 
make out, had been taken by young 
Stone-that-shines.. Remembering that 
he had made his marches on foot, and 
even taking it for granted that a young 
Indian runner covers much ground, we 
felt that he could not have kept pace 
with our horses. We were guided by 
that, trying to figure out our marches 
according to the scale of an Indian in 
rapid-traveling trim. And so we felt 
that about three days out we should 
come to the vicinity of the great river 
of which Stone-that-shines had spoken. 

Of course, we were worried. And 
even The Colonel’s optimism began to 
fade a bit as we put league after league 
of the journey behind us. But, as he 
put it, Stone-that-shines could not be 
wrong. He had floated north on a great 
river, and he had found a white moun- 
tain. There wasn’t any doubt about 
that. And the only thing that was to 
worry us was that we could not be quite 
sure about the warrior’s estimates of 
time. 

Time is only a comparative thing to 
an Indian. He will usually tell you that 
it is a long time when he is unhappy, 
and a short time when he is gay, though 


88 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


there may be more years in the happy 
time than days in the sad one. Who 
could tell exactly what had gone on in 
the starved and fever-burned brain of 
that youngster when he was scouting 
across the prairie according to the dic- 
tates of a dream? 

He himself had returned to the 
ground and striven to find that same 
river. And, in spite of his memory of 
the place, had failed utterly to find a 
great river floating to the northward. 
That didn’t hold out very brilliant 
hopes for us, and, of course, it con- 
vinced Stone-that-shines that the whole 
affair had been the product of a dream 


—that the stone had been placed in his 


hands by the blessed spirits. 


That was all very well for him, but - 


we were whites, and we couldn’t give 
quite so great leeway to the ghostly 
things on the prairie. We felt that 
there must be facts, and we were on the 
trail of a vein of gold of solid metal, 
as big as a man’s finger is wide. 


We erossed two or three creeks be- 


fore we were more than thirty miles 
away from the camp, but that was all 
the sign of water flowing north that we 
had during the first two days of our 
march. For another day we plunged 
vainly ahead and found nothing what- 
ever in the shape of a northward-flow- 
ing water. We knew that we would 
have to change our tactics, and so we 
sat down for a war council. 


CHAPTER XXVI. 
AN UNWELCOME VISITOR. 


WE eouldn’t be really downhearted 

on this night. There was too fine 
an air stirring, and the sky was as clear 
as polished glass. Whether you wanted 
to loek east toward the purple heavens, 
er west toward the red skies as they 
died, everything was beautiful, and 


when the lower world turned black the 


heavess whitened with stars. 
“We must go en,” said The Colonel 


dreamily, as he smoked his pipe and 
watched the little, twisted wreaths rise 
above his .head, where they were 
knocked to nothingness by the wind. 
“We must go on, my lad. Obviously 
we haven’t passed the right river, as‘ 
yet. And Stone-that-shines may have 
gone three times as far as he thought he 
went. Days mean nothing to those 
young scamps of Indians. We’ll go on 
until we really hit a white mountain, 
eh?” 

“I don’t know,” said I. 

“You don’t know what?” 

“I don’t know about going on.” 

“Find the answer by reductio ad ab- 
surdum,” said The Colonel. We have 
covered the ground that is behind us. 
The river is not there. Accordingly, 
we must go on.” 

I shook my head. 

“Look here, Christy,” said The Colo- 
nel, “you’re disagreeing with me sim- 
ply on principle.” 

“On principle, sir?” 

“Just because you think that I’m gen- 
erally wrong.” 

As a matter of fact, that was about 
it. The Colonel had a talent for making 
up his mind the wrong way, but still it 
was hard for me to see anything wrong 
in what he had said this evening. 

He went on: “Water flowing north. 
That's what we want. Haven't seen a 
sign of that, you know.” 

“We passed a creek that was run- 
ning northeast,” said I. 

“Stuff!” said he. “A mere trickle of 
water! Not enough to float a raft such 
as Stone-that-shines spoke of!“ 

“It wouldn’t have taken a big raft to 
float him,” I said. 

“Now, Christy,” said The od, 

“you know that you’re talking through 
your hat. If J had suggested that, you

brave and confess.” 

That was true. If he had suggested 
it, I would have thought it a pretty silly 
idea. However, there was a difference

Look what you've done!“ I said to

Watch her make herself at home!“

What could I do? 

She was calmly stripping the saddle 
from her horse, which she then hob-

her pack, she took out some food and

shot or trapped a rabbit, and now she

All this time she made not the slight-

You would have thought that she hardly 
guessed we were within many miles of

“Oh, Christy,” said The Colonel,

gel, when the news of this gets back

sense. But you’ve got to give her

I knew that I’d have to go. Besides, 
if he went alone, he’d be too apt to

pretty to keep from making a grown 
man a bit giddy. But I was a boy— 
and I wanted to keep my scalp where it

Ompah, where it was pretty sure to

As we stood up, I said to The Colo- 
nel: When Ompah and the rest of 
them get wind of this they’ll come hop-

them!” 

The Colonel merely stiffened. “Don’t 
put it on that footing,” said he. We're 
not to be driven to do our duty, I hope. 
I trust that we can do what is right 
without being whipped along by fear!” 

I saw that I had started off on the 
wrong foot, and so I said no more ex-

chiefly. What would become of her,

become of the rest of her life?” 

“Yes,” said The Colonel, “that’s very

poor girl! A lot better to turn her off

dark night. Seems hard, seems cruel to 
treat a poor girl like that. But what 
else is there for us to do?” 

He was getting up his courage and 
making himself a little more like steel. 
I only hoped that there would be 
plenty of it in him. But I doubted. I 
was afraid ef that Indian. She was too 
smart and too pretty to be trusted. Be- 


sides, there was about as much firmness

“My dear,” said he to The Quail,

prised to see you out here.” 

She looked up at him, scanning him

page. Then she looked back to her 
cooking. Any other woman in the 
world would have had something to say, 
but she knew all that silence can do— 
which is a great deal more than words 
can ever manage, of course. 

I nudged The Colonel, and he 
growled back at me: “How can I talk 
when she doesn’t even look at us?” 

“You’ve got to,” said J. 

“Look here,” said The Colonel heav- 
ily, “you know that this won’t do. The 
Mandans would not be happy if they 
knew that their young women were go-

„Oh!“ said The Quail, and she 
turned her head and looked at me cu-

strong language before he got through. 

Besides, said The Colonel, Ompah 
is a great friend of mine.”

“Will you answer me?” asked The 
Colonel. : 

At that she looked up at him more 
curiously than ever. “Are you angry?” 

I'm surprised—and——” 

“You are hungry,” said The Quail. 
“But this will be good for you, and 
after that you will talk more happily.” 

And she handed him the ramrod, 
loaded with fragrantly roasted rabbit!

CHAPTER XXVII. 
AN INDIAN MAID. | 


HE was a clever one! I never had 
doubted that there was a great deal 
under her silences, but now I was surer 
of it than ever. I had the proof now. 
Of course, this would have been noth-

and its load of meat, but he couldn’t

should avoid doing this. I suppose he 
thought that it would have been too im- 
polite to refuse anything that a lady 
offered to him. He took the spit and 
tasted a bit of the meat—automatically. 

“Don’t touch the stuff!” said I. 

But I was too late. It was already 
in his mouth, though he handed the 
ramrod back to her at once. She 
wouldn’t take it. 


“The Quail,” said she, “is not a child - 


or a fool. She does not taste food un- 
til the chief has eaten his fill. Besides, 
I have more meat, here. There is 
enough for both, unless the chief is 
very hungry!” 

She got up and fetched another rab- 
bit, cleaned and ready for roasting. 

The Colonel shook his head. “I have 
eaten,” he explained.’ “I do not wish 
to eat.“ 

The Quail took back the rod with a

She's able to take care of herself,”

soft as down. 

Then she faced him again, and

and smiling. I never saw another In- 
dian woman who dressed her hair in

He did not seem to hear me. “There 
was a touch of gold in it, I thought. 
Like the gold on her skin, Christy. It

heard of an Indian with golden-brown 
hair, Christy?” 

I didn’t like it a bit. 
knew just what to say.

“The chief is standing.”

red-hot coal out of the fire and brought

That was his way—just contrary !

fell.

of her hand I had removed, but I 
couldn’t remove the touch of her eyes 
or the velvet of her voice. 

“Look here, said I, if J started 
to explain things to you, you wouldn’t 
want to understand, and even if you 
did understand, it wouldn’t make any 
difference to you. 
some good advice right out of my 
heart. Will you listen to it?” 

“The Quail sits in the shadow of the 
wise man and hears him speak and 
wonders at him,” said she. 

I gave her a side look, but she didn’t 
seem to be smiling. I went on: “Very 
well, I'll tell you what I think you 
ought to do. Make up that pack of 
stuff that you brought to Standing 
Elk. Put the saddle back on the horse 
that you stole along with the other 
things, and cart them back to the Man- 
dans as fast as you can. They may be 
cross at you for carrying off so much 
loot from them. But they'll admire 
you and praise you when you show that 
you had the nerve to steal it—and then 
the generosity to bring it back. You 
take all of that stuff away from this 
camp and snake it along to the Man- 
dans, and when you get there, you leave 
the memory of Standing Elk farther 
behind you than you've left this fire. 
Let him go out of your head. Marry 
Ompah—he’s a great chief. Or marry 
anybody else that you have a mind to. 
But leave Standing Elk alone, because 
you can’t mix up the white chief and 


But I'll give you 


the Indian girl. It won't do. You un- 
derstand me?” 

She wasn’t pleased. I could see that 
my words hadn’t won anything from 
her. 

“Very well!” said she, and she stood 
up and snapped her fingers in my face. 

“What do you mean by that?” I 
asked her. 

“You are very strong with Standing 
Elk,” said this girl to me. “You speak 
and you draw him this way, and you 
speak and you draw him that way. 
However, I shall be strong, also. We 
will see who is the stronger, since you 
will not be my friend with him!” 

That was it. The clever little tiger 
had played her cards and hoped to win. 
She had fost, and so she threw up the 
bluff right away and let me see her as 
she was—dangerous, mighty danger- 
ous! I knew then, as I looked at her, 
that my throat would never be safe 
from her knife, so long as she was 
with us, 

“That’s the way it should be, then,” 
said I. “I like you very well because 
you're brave, and I like you because 
you're fond of my friend, Standing 
Elk—but you're a thief and a slippery 
bad one, from my way of thinking. I 
wish you bad luck. And I’m going to 
give you all of the bad luck that I can 
manage to bring your way!” 

So I turned my back on the camp fire 
and walked off into the blackness, hop- 
ing that she’d fall into the fire and 
turn into a cinder by the time that I 
got back to it. 

I went on until I saw the looming 
bulk of The Colonel before me. He 
was striding up and down, up and 
down, just beyond the light of the fire, 
and with every step that he took he 
was cursing. 

“All right, sir,” said I to him, “I’ve 
come to report. 

“She won't take the hint,” said I. 
“She won't leave us, even after you 
walked away from the fire.” 


96 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


“What hint?” asked The Colonel. 
“What are you talking about?” 

“I mean,” I explained, “that you'll 
have to go back and tell her in so many 
words, out and out, that she has to 
pack up and leave!” 


CHAPTER XXIX. 
` CHASE, 


II. looked awkward to The Colonel, 

but he was, about to give in and 
start back, I know, when a gun 
boemed across the silence of the prairie 
and a bullet divided the distance be- 
tween his head and mine as neatly as 
you please. We dropped for the 
ground, The Colonel grunting: “Give 
me your gun, Christy!” 

I shoved the butt of my revolver into 
his hand. At the same instant, the 
glow of the fire behind us went out 
as suddenly as a winking eye, and The 
Golonel, as he poised the gun and 
looked around him, growled: “Thank 
heavens for that! They won't have the 
light to see us by! But how did the 
girl get the fire out so quickly?” 

It was a good deal of a miracle to 
greenhorns like us. Of course, she 
had simply thrown a buffalo robe over 
the flame. That stifled the fire, though 
it ruined a good robe. But though 
there was no candle being held, now, 
by which they could see to murder us, 
we were not in very good shape. How 
many Indians might be out yonder in 
the dark we could not guess. But there 
were probably quite a few. Here we 
were, cut off from the fire, where our 
guns were. And even if nothing else 
were accomplished, we could be turned 
adrift on the plains with nothing but a 
single Colt and its six shots between 
us and a wretched death. 

A shadow moved dimly in the dis- 
tance, and The Colonel tried a pot shot 
at it, getting a half-stifled yelp of pain 
for his answer. That, if we knew any- 
thing about Indians, would make them 


pretty careful. The Colonel was big 
medicine, too, and that would count in 
his favor and make them more slow 
and cautious. In fact, if he hadn’t had 
such a repute, they would undoubtedly 
have rushed us at once and swept us 
out at the first charge. 

There was a murmug behind us—a 
voice just barely loud enough to come 
to our ears, and here was The Quail, 
with her arms full of guns. She had 
slipped along from the fire the minute 
that she hooded it, and now matters 
were changed a good deal. From hav- 
ing one revolver between us, we now 
had a rifle and a revolver apiece, to- 
gether with a reserve gun in the hands 
of The Quail. After what had been 
before us, for a moment, this was like 
absolute safety and release from that 
danger. 

The Colonel blessed The Quail in his 
own language and then in hers. But 
she didn’t seem to hear him. She 
merely said: “Standing Elk waits here 
for the rest of the party to come. He 
wishes to make a great slaughter of the 
Mandans!” 

“Are they Mandans?” asked The 
Colonel. 

“Did you not hear the voice?” said 
The Quail. “Yes, it is a Mandan who 
tried to kill you. Now, of course, he 
has gone back to bring up all the 
others!“ 

That explained everything with per- 
fect satisfaction. One Mandan, of the 
band which had been sent out, had 
scouted along and come across the fire. 
Instead of going back secretly and at 
once to warn the rest of the band of 
the thing he had found, he had tried 
to get himself glory and a great scalp 
by murdering The Colonel at once. 
Instead, he had collected a bullet for 
his pains. But now he was off, scoot- 
ing as fast as his horse could go. 

“Child,” said The Colonel, “you have 
more wit in your little finger than we 
have in our whole stupid bodies. Of

We did start moving, and we went

We should have made up our packs 
and got away as clean as a whistle had 
it not been for a most wonderful piece 
of honest foolishness on the part of 
The Colonel. He stopped by the dead 
fire and insisted to The Quail that she 
must leave him and cut off through the

village. 

What did she do? Just the eppo- 
site of what any man might have ex- 
pected. She simply wrapped a robe 
around her body and head and sat 
down ‘there, like a mummy. 

“They'll murder you when they 
come up!” cried The Colonel to her. 
“Murder, or worse.. You know that 
their blood will be up! Get up and run 
for it!” 

She wouldn’t stir, and she wouldn’t 
make so much as a whisper in answer. 
So The Colonel, with a groan, threw 
open her pack and serted it as well as 
he could by the starlight. I had things 


ready and begged him to come along. 


But he remained there, sorting, deelar- 
ing that he would have to sort out of 
the girl’s pack everything that she had 
stolen from the Mandans! 

Knowing that arguing would. simply 
make matters worse, I slipped off Kitty 
and ran to help in that sorting, and so 
we got down to the handful of stuff 
that might reasonably belong to The 
Quail. The rest we put in a heap, and 
left behind us the pony, too, which The 
Quail had brought aleng. ‘Then he 
scooped her up and put her on the lead 
horse. 

She didn't ask any questions; she 


didn’t protest that she would just as 


soon remain behind there and let the 
Indians murder her when they came. 
In fact, she did nothing that a sulky 
white girl might have dene. ‘Instead, 
she gave one wild, little choked cry of 


97, 


happiness, and then she scudded off 
across the prairies behind us. 

There was still no sign of the Man- 
dans behind us. The Colonel was for 
heading straight, but since that was 
the direction that they would reason- 
ably expect us to take, I begged him 
to drive straight back toward the site 
of their city on the river. He con- 
sented, though he said that we were 
simply running our heads into the 
lion’s mouth, 

However, we had half an hour of 
steady plugging, without a sign of In- 
dians riding up behind us. Then we 
stopped to. rearrange the packs, which 
had worked loose during our hasty gal- 
lop. The horses needed breathing, too. 
Owing to The Colonel’s confounded 
sense of what was right and just, we 
now had a hundred and fifty extra 
pounds to distribute over eur three 
horses, who already had all that they 
wanted to carry. 

We kept forging away all through 
the night. I knew that these Indians, 
even the children, have a wonderful in- 
stinct for direction, and to keep us 
from riding in a circle I got The Golo- 
nel to let The Quail establish our line 
of march. 

She rode a length or so ahead of us, 
and that was a good thing in another 
way, because The Colonel and I were 
frightened enough to hurry those 
horses beyond their strength. The 
Quail had enough Indian instinct to 
make haste slowly. She kept the 
marching down to a jog trot, mest of 
the time, and when we came te a rare 
hill, now and then, she let her horse 
walk! 

It made us nervous, of course, but 
we had to admit that it was probably 
wise to keep some strength fer run- 
ning in our mounts. 

As soon as dawn came, The Quail 
halted. She started unsaddling the 
tired horse that she was on, and then 
we saw a little, trickling stream of 


98 WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


water close ahead of us. With the sad- 
dle off, The Quail led her horse into 
the water and began to slosh double 
handfuls over it. Then she took it out 
and rubbed it fairly dry. After that 
she wrapped herself in a blanket and 
lay down, tying the horse to the sad- 
dle which was under her head. It 
could graze, in this fashion. If the 
tired girl fell asleep, the first snort or 
jerk of the horse would surely waken 
her. s 

Of course, we did not wait to see all 
of this before we started to follow her 
example. After the long march of the 
day before, this night business had 
about finished our mounts. They were 
too tired to even look at the grass, but 
the cold bath cooled and refreshed them 
a wonderful lot, and when we lay 
down, the trio was munching fodder 
and laying up the strength which we 
were pretty sure to need in our service 
that day. 

That halt was two hours. The Colo- 
nel told The Quail and me to sleep, 
that he would watch, but when I was 
wakened it was. by The Quail, and her 
tired eyes showed that she had done 
the double duty. The Colonel lay on 
his side with his head pillowed on his 
arm, snoring soundly. And The Quail 
already had a saddle on her mount. 

When she pointed to the sun I 
didn’t need to be told that it was time 
to march. In five minutes we were on 
our way again, and it was a ridiculous 
thing to watch The Colonel apologizing 
for having fallen asleep at his post— 
and he a soldier! It was stranger still 
to see the way in which The Quail took 
this apologizing. She said: 

“Why should not the squaw watch 
when the chief sleeps? It is her hap- 
piness !” 

The Colonel was silenced. He cast 


a wretched glance at me, and I glow- 
ered back at him. The more I saw of 
The Quail, the more clearly I could tell 
that she was a rare one, indeed—a 
jewel of her kind, if there ever was 
such a thing among Indians. She was 
as brave as steel, as true as a sword, 
and prettier, in her own way, than 
Martha Farnsworth would ever be— 
even when a painter tried to idealize 
her. 

Still, the thing couldn’t be. And the 
more I realized that The Quail was a 
queen, the more miserable the whole 
affair made me. I blamed The Colo- 
nel a lot, too. Not that he did anything 
absolutely wrong, but he certainly 
should never have let her settle herself 
beside our fire. If he wanted to leave 
her there, he should have carted himself 
and me away and forbade her to fol- 
low. That was the only way out of 
the bad corner that we were in about 
The Quail. 

However, here we were, riding for 
our lives, and the girl showing us most 
of the ways in which those lives could 
be saved. Nothing could have been 
worse! 

We went on at a steady march all 
that morning, only making three short 
pauses, and these were all at the sug- 
gestion of The Quail. For when we 
came to big stretches of rock, three 
times in a row, she made us slow up 
and follow her, while she rode in cir- 
cles through the rocks and laid down 
a trail problem which might make 
some Mandan brains ache a bit before 
the nightfall. 

Perhaps it was nothing but these 
that delayed the band so long, but the 
sun was hanging deep in the west be- 
fore the dust cloud which we had been 
dreading showed on the edge of the 
sky.

THE ENGLISH BULLDOG 


We) OME time ago I told you 
in these pages about that 
grand old English dog, 
the mastiff. You remem- 
ber, do you not, that the 
4 mastiff is powerful and 

me From that breed the Eng- 

lish bulldog probably was derived sev- 
eral hundreds of years ago. 
As the name suggests, the bulldog 


was pitted against the bull in fights at- 


tended at first by the nobility of Eng- 
land and for several hundred years by 
some other classes. The cruel pleasure 
of bull baiting is happily a thing of the 
past, but it may not be amiss to describe 
the practice, which is responsible for 
the English bulldog of to-day. 

A rope about fifteen feet leng was 
attached to the bull’s horns and tethered 
him to a stake. The bull tried to kill 
the deg by goring it or trampling it; 
the dog attempted to sink its teeth into 
the bull’s nese and hold on till the ani- 
mal was exhausted frem its own efforts 
and loss. of blood. For the purpeses 
of this “sport,” a dog with an under- 
shot jaw and a nose set back in the face 
could hang on best to the bull and 
breathe mest easily. 

In 1835 bull baiting was prohibited. 
Then breeders began to exaggerate the 
distinctive characteristics of the Eng- 
lish bulldog until they made it into a 
canine monstrosity. If the bulldog of 


to-day were matched against the bull, 
the almost-sure winner would be the 
bull. 

You would judge, perhaps, in looking 
at the bulldog, that he was ferocious, 
only awaiting a good opportunity. to 
spring at you. In reality the bulldog is 
sweet tempered. Courage and tenacity 
he has to a great degree and when he 
is aroused will fight tp the death. 

Here is the standard of the Bulldog 
Club of America: 

General Appearance, Attitude, Ex- 
pression, et cetera.—The perfect bull- 
dog must be of medium size and smooth 
coat; with heavy, thickset, low-swung 


‘body, massive, short-faced head, wide 


shoulders afid sturdy limbs. The gen- 
eral appearance and attitude should sug- 
gest great stability, vigor, and strength. 
The disposition should be equable and 
kind, resolute, and courageous—not 
vicious or aggressive—and the demeanor 
should be pacific and dignified. Dis- 
position is indicated hy the expression 
and behavior of the dag in the ring. 
Gait.— The style and carriage are pe- 
cutiar, the gait being a loose-jointed, 
shuffling, sidewise metion, giving the 
characteristic “roll.” The action must, 
however, be free and vigorous. 
Proportion and Symmetry.—The 
“points” should be well distributed and 
bear good relation one te the ether, no 
feature being in such preminence from 


100 


either excess or lack of quality that the 
animal appears deformed or illy pro- 
portioned. 


Influence of Sex.—In comparison of- 


specimens of different sex, due allow- 
ance should be made in favor of the 
bitches, which do not bear the char- 
acteristics of the breed to the same de- 
gree of perfection as do the dogs. 

Size.—The size for mature dogs is 
about fifty pounds; for mature bitches 
about forty pounds. 

Coat.— Straight, short, flat, close, of 
fine texture, smooth and glossy. No 
fringe, feather, or curl. 

Color of Coat.— Uniform, pure of its 
kind, and brilliant. The various colors 
found in the breed are to be preferred 
in the following order: 1—red brindle, 
2—all other brindles, 3—solid white, 
4—solid red, fawn, or fallow, 5—pie- 
bald, 6—inferior qualities of all the 
foregoing. A perfect piebald is pref- 
erable to a muddy brindle or defective 
solid color. Solid black is undesirable, 
but black is not so objectionable if oc- 
curring to a moderate degree in pie- 
bald bitches. The brindles to be per- 
fect should have a fine, even, and equal 
distribution of the composite colors. In 
brindles and solid colors a small white 
patch on the chest is not considered 
detrimental. In piebalds the color 
patches should be well defined, of pure 
color and symmetrically distributed. 

Skin.—Seft and loose, especially at 
the head, neck, and shoulders. 

Wrinkles and Dewlap.— The head 
and face should be covered with heavy 


wrinkles, and at the throat, from jaw to 


chest, there sheuld be two loose pen- 
dulous folds, forming the dewlap. 
Skull. Should be very large; in cir- 
cumference, in front of the ears it 
should measure at least the height of 
the dog at the shoulder. Viewed from 
the front it should appear very high 
from the corner of the lower jaw to the 
apex of the skull, and also very broad 
and square. Viewed at the side, the 


WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


head should appear very high, and very 
short from the point of the nose to. 
the occiput. 

The forehead should be flat, not 
rounded or. “domed ;” neither too prom- 
inent nor overhanging the face. 

Cheeks.—Well rounded, protruding 


‘ sideways and outward beyond the eyes. 


Stop.—The temples or frontal bones 
should be very well defined, broad, 
square, and high, causing a hollow or 
groove between the’eyes. The inden- 
tation or stop should be both broad and 
deep, extending up the middle of the 
forehead, dividing the head vertically, 
and traceable to the top of the skull. 

Eyes and Eyelids. Seen from the 
front the eyes should be situated low 
down in the skull, as far from the 
ears as possible. Their corners should 
be in a straight line, at right angles 
with the stop. They should be 
set quite in front of the head and as 
wide apart as possible, provided their 
outer corners are within the outlines of 
the cheeks when viewed from the front. 
Neither sunken nor bulging, they should 
be round, of moderate size, very dark 
in color. When the dog is looking di- 
rectly forward the lids should cover the 
white of the eyeball, showing no haw. 

Ears. Should be set high in the 
head, the front inner edge of each ear 
joining the outline of the skull at the 
top back corner of skull, so as to place 
them as wide apart, and as high, and 
as far from the eyes as possible. Small, 
thin, and “rose ear” in shape. The rose 
ear folds inward at its back lower edge, 
the upper front edge curving over, out- 
ward and backward, showing part of 
the inside bur. The ears should not be 
carried erect or “prick-eared” or “but- 
toned” and should never be cropped. 
A button ear is one that falls forward 
close to the side of the skull. 

Face.—Measured from the front of 
the cheek bone to the top of the nose, 
the face should be extremely short, the 
muzzle being very short, broad, turned 


YOUR DOG 


upward, and very deep from the corner 
of the eye to the corner of the mouth. 

Nose.—Large and broad, its tip 
should be set back deeply between the 
eyes. The distance from bettom of 
stop, between the eyes, to the tip of 
nese should be as short as possible; it 
should not exceed the length from the 
top of nose to the edge of underlip. 
The nostrils should be wide, large and 
black, with a well-defined line between 
them. Any nose ether than black is 
ebjectionable, and “Dudley” or flesh- 
colored nose is absolutely e 
from competition. 

Chops.— The chops or flews should 
be thick, broad, pendant, and very deep, 
completely overhanging the lower jaw 
at each side. They join the upper lip 
in front and almost or quite cover the 
teeth, which should be scarcely notice- 
able when the moutli is closed. 


Jaws. Massive, very broad, square, 
and undershot, the lower jaw projecting 


considerably in front of the upper jaw 
and turning up. 

Teeth—Large and strong, with the 
canine teeth or tusks wide apart, and 
the six small teeth in front, between 
the canines, in an even, level row. 

Neck.—Short, very thick, deep, and 
strong, well arched at the back. 

Shoulders——Muscular, very heavy, 
widespread, and slanting outward, giv- 
ing stability and great power. 

Chest.—Very broad, deep, and full. 

Brisket and Body.—The body should 
be very capacious, with full sides and 
well-rounded ribs. It sheuld be well 
ribbed up behind, with the belly tucked 
up and not rotund where it jeins the 
chest, The brisket should be very deep 
from the shoulders down to its lewest 
part, where it joins the ribs. It should 
be well let down between the sheulders 
and forelegs, giving the dog a bread, 
low, short-legged appearance. 

Back.—Short and strong, very bread 


101 


at the shoulders, and comparatively nar- 
row at the loins. There should be a 
slight fall in the back, close behind the 
shoulders—its lowest part—whence the 
spine should rise to the leins, the top of 
which should be higher than the top of 
the shoulders, thence curving again 
more suddenly to the tail, forming an 
arch—a very distinctive feature of the 
breed. 

Forelegs.—Short, very stout, straight, 
and muscular and set wide apart. The 
well-developed calves, present a bowed 
outline, but the bones of the legs should 
not be curved or bandy, nor the feet 


brought too close together. 


Elbews.—Should be low and stand 
well out and loose from the bedy. 

Hind Legs.—Strong, muscular, and 
lenger than the forelegs, so as to ele- 
vate the loins abeve the shoulders. 
Hecks should be slightly bent and well 
let down, so as to give length and 
strength from loins to hock. The lower 
leg should be short, straight and strong, 
with the stifles turned slightly outward 
and away from the body. The hocks 
are therefore made to approach each 
other, and the hind feet to turn out- 
ward. 

Feet. Moderate in size, compact, 
and firmly set. Tees compact, well split 
up, with high knuckles and shert and 
stubby nails. The front feet may be 


straight or slightly outturned, but the 


hind feet should be pointed well out- 
ward. l 

Tail.—Either straight or “screwed” 
but never curled nor curly. It must be. 
short, hung lew, with decided down- 
ward carriage, thick root, and fine tip. 
If straight, the tail sheuld be cylindrical 
and of uniform taper. If “screwed,” 
the bends or kinks should be well de- 
fined; they may be abrupt or even 
knetty, but no portien of the members 


should be elevated above the base or 


root.

Harley P. Lathrop 


Author of “With a Lariat for Fishline,” ete. 


N order to understand the 
opportunity which lay 
open before Steve Burk- 
hart, you must compre- 
hend this: During a pe- 
riod of protracted 
drought in a range country the rate of 
decrease among horse stock will, as a 
rule, exceed the shrinkage in cattle. 
This follows as a natural sequence, for 
not only do horses require more grass 
but they will trample and destroy far 
more than they consume. So during a 
long dry spell, when every blade of 
fodder counts, the ranchers, as an act 
of self-protection, usually cut their 
supply of brood mares and young stock 
to the bone. 

The part of the State where Steve 
Burkhart lived had just experienced 
two years of drought. Now this was 
broken and the reaction was about to 


occur. Steve was the first to sense 
the acute shortage of horses. 

He was a tall, easy-spoken, even-dis- 
positioned young fellow with his eyes 
fixed on the main chance, which in his 
case meant a sizable brand of his own 
some day. A few years before, at- 
tracted by the increase in pay, he had 
forsaken cow-punching and a steady 
diet for the more remunerative, if 
somewhat more hazardous, occupation 
of bronc busting. So with his finger 
on the pulse of the market, so to speak, 
he was the first to realize the extreme 
scarcity of horses. ` l 

“If I only knew where I could pick 
up a good jag of young stuff I’d clean 
up a pot of money,” he confided to his 
friend, Tommy Baker. “Every outfit 
around here is just cryin’ for ridin’ 
stock.” 

Tommy had recently returned from 


ALL GENTLED 


Barataria County, where he had been 
in charge of a herd of cattle shipped 
there to pasture through the drought. 
“Then you go to Brides Corners, 
Steve,“ he advised. “It’s just full af 
horses around there. Every one wants 
to sell, and chances are you can buy 
worth the money.” 

So this was why Steve Burkhart 
happened to be traveling the highway 
not far from Brides Corners one mid- 
morning a few weeks later. He had 
chosen to make the journey overland 
by horseback, on the chance he might 
deal for some desirable young stuff en 
route. 

A mile back Steve had passed a 
large ranch house set well off the road. 
On the right of the highway lay a long, 
rectangular piece of open country. 
Just how wide the strip was Steve had 
no means of knowing, for on the oppo- 
site edge it merged into a belt of tim- 
ber. 
ning the timber, he caught sight of two 
moving objects just within the brush. 
He gazed intently, 

“A paint hoss with some one after 
him,” he muttered to himself. “They’re 
ramblin', too, and no mistake.” 

Shortly the paint swerved and, leav- 
ing the timber, took to the prairie, 
headed for the highway. A hundred 
yards in the rear followed the rider. 
Forty feet behind, little eruptions of 
grass and twigs marked the paint’s 
trail. Steve guessed these were kicked 
up by the end of a trailing lariat. 

The flame of the chase kindled in 
his eye, and, leaving the road, he sought 
concealment. behind a small shrub, 
meantime reaching for his rope: On- 
ward, straight toward him, thundered 
the paint. Not until a scant fifty 
yards away did the horse catch sight 
of Steve. Then he whirled at right 
angles to his former course. Steve 
dropped in behind. He could see quite 
plainly now. The dragging rope was 
attached to a heavy hackamore, and

Since then seen many owners. Now

A VETERAN INDIAN FIGHTER 


AMONG the interesting characters living at the Soldiers’ Home at Monte 
Vista, Colorado, is Jacob C. Harmon, who still gets enjoyment out of life 


at seventy-eight. 


and set out to find some of the adventure for himself. 
in a wagon drawn by an ox team in 1861, and visited Denver. 


Harmon was lured by the tales of the West at an early age 


He crossed the plains 
He recalls the 


Denver of that period as a village of shacks, mostly of dilapidated appearance. 
The principal building of those days was the Headley & Chase gambling hall, 
where money and whisky flowed freely. It was easy come, easy go. Harmon 
remembers seeing from fifty to sixty thousand dollars in gold at one time on 
the tables of this resort. Shooting from the hip was then a common practice, 
and card sharpers were frequently given swift justice when caught red-handed 
at their tricks. 

Harmon saw service in the Indian wars. For many years, he was a scout 
for General Nelson A. Miles. In 1864, he was in the Chivington massacre, on 
Sand Creek. He took part in the battles with the Cheyennes from 1863 to 
1864, ‘and was in the Smoky Hill massacre, when 107 out of 115 men were 
killed. The veteran recently paid a visit to Denver and found pleasure in contrast- 
ing the city as it is to-day with the cluster of shacks that it was when he 
first saw it. 


(Pocatello) Erle Wilson 


IEARS before the first 
white man came to the 
Idaho hills the Indians 
used to gather at a 
favored spot in the 
southeastern part of the 
State, to barter game and skins from 
the interior for dried fish from the 
coast. Some time later the route of the 
Old Oregon Trail passed through this 
Indian gathering place, and in 186# Ben 
Holladay made a settlement there in the 
form of a stage station on his Salt- 
Lake-Virginia-City-Montana line. This 
frontier qutpost was called Pocatello 
after Chief Pokatello of the Bannock 
Indian tribe. 

With the development of the great 
West, the little Idaho hamlet grew, the 
geographic advantages which had led 
the redskins to meet there making it a 
natural distributing center. To-day 
Pocatello is known as the “Gate. City” 
of Idaho. With a population of 15,001 
people, it is the second largest city of 


Author of “Pueblo,” ete. 
the State and the seat of Bannock 


County. Its town site comprises two 


thousand acres of land, originally sold 
by the Indians to the United States. 
As the principal center of the Oregon 
Short Line, Pocatello is the home of the 
largest railroad plant west of Omaha, 
with buildings and equipment which 
cost from ten to fifteen million dollars. 
Direct connection with eleven States is 
afforded. The railroad has just completed 
a new two-million-dellar ice plant and 
tie-treating plant. The switching yards 
are being enlarged and additions to the 
shops built. Approximately 3,000 railroad 
employees live here, the monthly pay 
toll reaching $450,000. The fourth 
largest Railroad Young Men’s Chris- 
tian Association in the world is located 
in Pocatello. 
Situated in the midst of the. fine 
grazing land of Bannock County, Po- 
catello is an important live-stock cen- 
ter. It is also a large wholesale center, 
an annual. business of eleven million dol- 


116 


lars being carried on. Rich agricul- 
tural lands surround this Idaho city, 
a giant reclamation project now irrigat- 
ing 75,000 tributary acres. To the 
southeast lies the Fort Hall mining dis- 
trict. 

This city is a pleasant place in which 
to live, and along its well-paved streets 
are many attractive homes. The climate 
is ideal and the death rate low. Poca- 
tello has a Carnegie Library, a splendid 
school system, and is the seat of the 
Idaho Technical Institute. The United 
States has an erial landing field here. 
Pocatello lies within a day’s drive of the 
most diversified scenic wonderland of 
the West, and for the convenience of 
tourists the city maintains a camp for 
motorists. 

Just outside the city is old Fort Hall, 
one of the most interesting historic land- 
marks west of the Rocky Mountains. 
This frontier outpost started life as a 
block house, built by Captain Wyeth, 
who had been sent out from Boston in 
the early 30s to carry supplies to the 
hunters in the region. The Hudson’s 
Bay Company interfered with his plans, 
however, and he was obliged to camp 
on the banks of the Snake River. On 
the site of the stockade which he built, 


WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


the United States government after- 
ward erected a military post, which was 
called Fort Hall in honor of the Bos- 
tonian who had outfitted Captain Wyeth 
for his expedition. ` Jason Lee, a pio- 
neer Methodist minister, preached at 
Fort Hall in 1832, at which time the 
Stars and Stripes were first raised over 
the post. The Bannock Indians now 
live upon the Fort Hall Reservation. 
There is under construction at the 
present time at American Falls, twenty- 
five miles west of Pocatello, a dam 
which when completed will store a large 
volume of water, submerging the site 
of Old Fort Hall. A shaft of native 


marble with a beacon light is being 


planned as a memorial for this historic 
spot. 

Pocatello operates under the laws of 
the State of Idaho as a first-class city. 
The Honorable C. Ben Ross is the pres- 
ent mayor. Among the city's promi- 
nent citizens are Walter H. Cleare, Ida- 
ho’s pioneer merchant; Carl A. Valen- 
tine, president of the First National 
Bank; R. A. Pierce, general superin- 
tendent of the Oregon Short Line Rail- 
road; and A. C. Henckley, superintend- 


ent of motive power and machinery of 


the Oregon Short Line Railroad.

REDWOOD TREE AS CACHE 


VIOLATORS of the law have found a new use for the huge redwood trees 

of the California forests. This was recently brought to light when prohibition 
agents found a redwood tree six miles from Dyerville, that had been skillfully 
adapted to use as a shelter for an illicit moonshine still. The tree is twenty-four 
feet in diameter. A hollow chamber in its base was found to contain a fifty- 
gallon still in full operation. Kerosene was used as fuel, and the small quantity 
of smoke that resulted was carried off by an aperture a little way up and concealed 
by foliage. The entrance to this chamber was hidden by canvas painted to re- 
semble the bark of the tree. 

On making the discovery, the prohibition agents “padlocked” the tree by 
posting a notice to the effect that the aforesaid redwood had been outa under 
the ban of the law for its infractions thereof.

“NEZ PERCE” JONES PASSES ON 

THERE recently died at Philipsburg, Montana, aged eighty-two, an old pioneer 

known as “Nez Perce” Jones, or James H. Jones, In 1878, Jones was pros- 
pecting on placer ground in McKay’s Gulch on the west fork of Rock Creek, 
in company with three other men. At this time parties of Nez Perce braves: 
were making their way to the headquarters of the tribe in the wilds of Idahe. 
They were in no friendly state of mind toward the whites. One evening, a party 
of these hostile warriors entered McKay’s Gulch and encountered Jack Hays, 
one of the prospecting party. They found him outside his cabin, it is believed, 
and immediately set upon him, beating him to death with whip handles, His 
body was found, face down, in a dry gulch, a few hundred yards above the cabin. 

The Indians then followed the trail of the miners into the upper reaches 
of the gulch and entered a glade where they came upon the cabin occupied by 
Jones, and his two other companions, named Joy and Elliott. It was early morn- 
ing, and the prospectors were asleep. The Indians announced their presence by 
pounding on the door. The white men were unarmed, save for a couple of old 
muzzle-loading Hawkins rifles, that were in the cabin. 

Jones opened the door to the intruders, who said that they were ‘hungry. 
He invited them in and set about getting breakfast. Only three of the nineteen 
Indians, however, entered. Joy presently went out of the cabin, and as soon 
as he did so, a shot rang out, and he fell, seriously wounded. Jones, realizing 
that treachery was afoot, lost no more time, but dashed. out of the cabin and 
turned the corner of the dwelling leading in the direction of the stream. A 
young buck armed with a six-shooter tried to stop him, but Jones evaded him 
and plunged into the willows beside the stream and made for the dense timber. 
The Indians sent several shots after him, one of which inflicted a slight wound. 
Once, when pursuit became too close, Jones picked up a rock and threw it at the 
nearest Indian, hitting him on the gun arm and disabling that member. 

In a state of complete exhaustion, Jones reached Philipsburg, twenty miles 
or more away. There he got medical treatment and had a posse dispatched in 
pursuit of the redskins. This event came to be known as the McKay’s Gulch 
Massacre, and from his being the sole survivor, Jones acquired his title of Nez 
Perce Jones.

Down Upon the Rio Grande 


By James E. Hungerford 


HERE o’ late, I'm dreamin’ nightly 
O' that sweet, enchantin’ land, 
Where the sun is shinin’ brightly, 
Down upon the Rio Grande. 

I can hear the birds a-singin’, 
Through the fabric o my dream, 
As beside that stream they’re wingin’, 
Where the golden ripples gleam. 


ELT 


I can sniff the scent o’ roses, 
Blewn upon the evenin' breeze, 

An’ the perfume o' wild posies, 
An’ the fragrance o’ pine trees. 

I can see the ol’ moon shinin’ 
Softly on that magic land, 

An’ my heart is jest a-pinin’ 
Fer the silv’ry Rio Grande. 

`I can hear the tides a-flowin’ 
Swiftly onward to the sea, 

An’ the gentle breezes blowin’ 
Sing a symphony to me. 

Up above the stars are gleamin’, 
Like great diamonds in the skies; 

T can see them.in my dreamin’, 
As I’ve seen em with my eyes. 


I can picture that ol’ river, 
Rushin’ swiftly on its ways, 

An’ it makes my heartstrings quiver 
With the dreams o' other days; 

On its shores, I’ve spent weeks campin’, 
That I never can forget; 

Long its course, long days o' trampin', 
That come back to haunt me yet. 

In the eve, when dusk is fallin', 
An' the day is takin' flight, 

I can hear that river callin’, 
Callin' softly through the night. 

An' I can’t keep back the longin’ 
Any longer, fer that land! 

I must go, where I’m belongin’— 

Down upon the Rio Grande!

He Changed 


His Handle 


BY Howard E.Morgan 


Author of “The Houn’ Dog Earns His Keep,” ete. 


R|HET’S me, right enough, 
‘No-luck’ Winton. Forty 
year of it. Don’t seem 
possible thet my luck has 
bruk. Got to, some time, 
though. Dang it all, I 
gotta strike it rich, some time. Nary a 
feller ever lived whut didn’t strike it 
lucky oncet in a while. Me, I ain’t never 
done thet, yit. Bout time, though. 
Yassir, it’s about time.” 

Inasmuch as No-luck was talking to 
himself no reply was forthcoming. He 
stumbled on through the purple dusk, a 
stooped, brown-drab, shambling figure, 
his crooked legs holding with difficulty 
to the narrow, rock-strewn trail. 

During forty years, Jed Winton had 
prospected through the Vanada Hills 
with conspicuous lack of success. As 
No-luck Winton he was known from 
‘one end of Clinton County to the other. 
For the better part of this well-nigh 
half-century span, his efforts had con- 


NWA, 


centrated on a search for Lost Cabin 
Mine. And now—he thought he had 
found it. Naturally optimistic, he was 
inwardly sure of it, as he had been sure 
a dozen times before—only to be dis- 
appointed in the end. Circumstances al- 
ways seemed. to conspire against him. 
This particular case was no exception 
to the rule. For months he had followed 
along an old stream bed, years since 
turned from its course. The farther 
along this stream he went, the more 
color he found, and the harder it became 
to get at. As he progressed, he had 
come upon traces of old workings. And 
finally, when within fifty yards of his 
goal—that point where two streams had 
merged—tons of rock, pounded into 
cementlike hardness by action of the 
weather, combated completion of his 
task. It would mean a month, two 
months, of back-breaking labor to clear 
away that rock. -And money. He was 
an old man, crippled with rheumatism. 


126 


And he had no money. That is, not 
much. He did have about five hundred 
dollars in dust cached down there in the 
little cabin, however. More money than 
he had ever had before when confronted 
by situations of this same sort. He 
counted this the one favorable sign; he 
wouldn’t have to borrow, now, for a 
couple of months at least. Borrowing 
was easy from his many friends in 
Wellsville. The payment of these easily 
acquired debts; however, was something 
else again. And he dared not take on 
any heavy obligation—now. Each year 
he confidently expected to be his last 
in the hills. Soon now, would come the 
time when he would be unable to earn 
the yearly grubstake, let alone pay any 
unusual debts. : Of course, there was al- 
ways the chance that he would strike it 
tich. It was bound to happen. It had 
to. Each year this never-failing assur- 
ance carried him through six long 
months of futile, body-breaking toil, and 
six more of endless planning. Each 
spring, oftentimes by starving himself 
through the winter months, he headed 
for the hills with his old burro, Jimps, 
heavily laden with supplies and equip- 
ment. 

This particular day he had worked 
hard. The little cabin was a mile from 
the object of his present interest. The 
trail was a rough one, and rather than 
take old Jimps away from the patch of 
lush grass near the cabin he had made 
the trip alone, much as he enjoyed his 
long-eared friend’s ever-placid com- 
panionship. Skirting the ridge top 
above the cabin, he hurried his pace in 
response to a distant peal of thunder. 
Jimps didn’t like thunderstorms. He 
must reach camp before the shower 
broke. She was all right when he was 
there. Reg lar old baby, Jimps was. 


Muttering, he slipped and slid recklessly - 


down the steep ridge wall. 

In a flash of lightning the little cabin 
was outlined, briefly, against the back- 
ground of black-trunked alders. And

flat on his face. The shots swished

In the ensuing blackness the old man

When it was again dark, the old man 
made his way to the cabin, strapped on 
hiş gun, and calmly set about preparing 
supper. 

Misfortune was no stranger to No- 
luck Winton. During forty years he 
had encountered this dreary trailmate 
in many guises. His money was gone 
as was most of his food. But he did 
not rant and rave. His kindly gray eyes 
held a hint of sadness for a moment, 
but for a moment only. Then he set 
about busily preparing for a trip to 
town. He needed money and supplies, 
and he would get them somehow. He 
must get away at once. No time was 
to be lost. Every hour was precious if 
he hoped to uncover Lost Cabin Mine 
before winter. 


It was gray dawn when he plodded 
wearily along the muddy main street of 
Wellsville. The storm had apparently 
centered here. The irregular, trench- 
like gutters ran deep with swift, brown 
water. Bright lights shone from the 
Miner’s Rest. Dark figures straggled 
toward the hills through the gray drizzle 
—miners bound for the Spiegel Mines. 
The far end of the town, containing the 
business district, was in darkness. Sud- 
denly flashes of light appeared in this 
darkness, followed closely by the roar 
of guns. The half dozen miners 
promptly scattered. No-luck stood still 
on the outer circle of light from the 
open door of the Miner’s Rest wonder- 
ing vaguely what it was all about. He, 
too, would have moved out of possible 
harm’s way, but there was no place to 
go. On one side was the roadway, knee- 


glimpsed the rider’s face. 


127 


deep with mud, on the other side a six-

were more shots, Galloping horses ap- 
proached through the sucking mud. 
No-luck calmly considered the advisa- 
bility of jumping the ditch. But no— 
he couldn’t make it, and he didn’t want 


.to get wet; bad for his rheumatism. 


A lone rider appeared in the circle of 
light. He was lashing his sweat-covered 
horse cruelly. No-luck teetered on the 
edge of the ditch to give the rider plenty 
of room. He had no intention of trying 
to stop the horseman. Just a little argu- 
ment following an all-night poker game 
probably. It was none ef his business. 
The horseman, looking back over a 
shoulder, was driving straight toward 
him. The old man yelled. The rider 
turned his head. A gun barrel gleamed 
dully in his hand. But he did not shoot. 
Instead, just as he passed the old man,

With dry clothes on his back and a

Various standing rewards, totaling close

Then he wouldn’t have to borrow.

No-luck slept soundly that night in

then
