RESTORING TOTEM POLES 
pD URIN G this summer, the historic and picturesque totem poles of the national 
monument at Kasaan, in southeastern Alaska, are to be preserved. from decay 


by the Smithsonian Institution. The totem pole was and still is an important 
factor in the lives of the Indians of the coast of Alaska and British Columbia. 
The figures carved on the totem pole generally represented the reputed animal 
ancestors of the owner. ‘While they usually resemble human beings, this is 
explained by the Indian belief that the animals had the power to take on human 
-form at will.. Distinguished visitors, too, are often immortalized by being placed 
on the totem pole of the Indian family of whom they are the guests. Thus, 
some years ago, a secretary of the interior visited an Indian dwelling while on 
a tour of Alaska; within a short time, the secretary in frock coat and high hat 
was represented on the totem pole of his host as a highly prized patron spirit. 

The totem pole was always erected in honor of the builder’s mother’s brother, 
from whom he inherited material possessions and family crests. The ashes 
of the uncle also were kept in the totem pole. The totem was not erected in 
the cemetery, but occupied the place of honor at the center of the gable end 
of the owner’s house. i 

The Indians carved their totem poles out of solid logs of white cedar. Of 
late years many of these totems have fallen into decay, and it is planned in 
the course of the present summer, to restore and reset all those at Kasaan that 
are preservable, on a cement base, scraping off the decayed wood, applying a 
coat of creosote, and adding a metal cap. In this way, it is hoped that these 
primitive monuments òf Indian culture will be preserved for many years to come. 


OMES William Meyers, old- 
timer of the plains, now at the 
National Home, Wisconsin. Got 

a little contrib to make, has Brother 
Meyers, and we certainly hope he'll 


come again and often, for he’s experi- . 


enced a lot of things in his time, the 

kind of things as are most interestin’ 

talk for us, who gather here, to listen to. 
People, William Meyers: 


“Boss AND Forks: I have been, so 
far, only a listener here at The Round- 
up, but as I have never heard this sub- 
ject mentioned here, nor have I ever 
read about it in ahy magazine or book, 
I thought you-all would be interested 


in listening to a little talk about the very 


first policemen of the plains, 
“Contrary to public belief, and re- 
gardless of how ‘wild’ they were, the 
villages of the old-time Indians of the 
plains—the banded-together-in-friend- 
ship people, as the Dakota Sioux were 
known among the Indians—were as well 
governed. and policed as any of our 
cities are to-day—sometimes more so! 
“Those men were selected by the 
chief of the village and were called ‘dog 
soldiers.’ Usually there was two lodges 
of them, and when in camp their lodges 


72 


flanked the lodge of the head chief. 
Upon them under the chief's orders 
rested the welfare of the village. They 
did the guard duty, policed the village 
and took care of all loose stock,’ that 
is, animals that had been taken in bat- 
tle or on a raiding expedition. 

“Those men were chosen for their 
ability. to use good, calm judgment and 
also for their popularity. Every band 
had its secret orders—just as do our 
college boys of to-day! Each order 
had its ‘skin lodge’ which could be 
called their ‘frat’ house. 

“The orders were issued by the chief 
to his dog soldier chief, and he was 
held legally responsible for the camp 
as a whole. Any crime that could be 
called a crime, such as stealing meat 
or other foodstuff from a member of 
the band, or refusing to camp in a cer- 
tain spot designated by the dog sol- 
diers, resulted in the culprit’s being 
‘soldier killed.’ 

“He was first given a sound drub- 
bing, then his hair was cut and his face 
and body painted black, and he was 
paraded, naked, before the village! 
Then everything he owned was con- 
fiscated and either divided among the 
villagers or destroyed. Then the 


134 


offender’s clothes, which had been filled 
with knife slits, were given back to him 
and he was chased, bodily, out of camp 
and away from the tribe entirely. There 
never was any chance for a pardon after 
the offender had been soldier killed. 
He, to his people, was dead. However, 
some of these unfortunates, if they did 
not seek some isolated spot and com- 
mit suicide, have been known to go to 
other tribes and eventually redeem 
themselves and become good citizens of 
the new tribe, but ever to venture to 
return to their home tribe meant death 
—sometimes death of an awful kind. 

“This law has been practiced and 
there is sign talk—paint symbols on 
buckskin—for as far back as three hun- 
dred and fifty years where it was used 
‘on the painted robe of the Sioux In- 
dian nation. 

“The old-time Indian was a pretty 
decent fellow when one knew him and 
told the truth about him.” 


That the “Indian was a pretty decent 
fellow,” voices a sentiment we've al- 
ways felt. We might add that we also 
feel that the Indian many times got a 
pretty rough deal from the white in- 
vaders, the slogan of many of whom 
was, “There is no good Indian but a 
dead Indian.” If you knew that an in- 
vading host felt that way about you 
and yours—well, how would you feel 
and what would you do?

Dear Miss Rivers: I have a small place of 
twenty-seven acres in Ontario, Canada, sixteen 
miles from a railroad, where there is the very 
best of hunting and fishing. I am looking for 
a partner to make the trip with me and stay 
for two or three weeks; would like some one 
who has a car. It would require very little to 
make this trip, as I will stand fifty per cent 
of all expenses. As I am expecting to go 
there again in September hunting moose I 
would like to start right after the Fourth of 
July. I am twenty-eight years old and pre- 
fer some one of my own age. So let’s see 
how many nature lovers there are in The Gang.

Dear Gx: I am a newly wed and, of 
course, have moved away from my friends, if 
such they could be called. None of them ever 
came to visit me when I was at home and sin- 
gle. Even those I went to school with. None 
ever invited me anywhere. I guess it is be- 
cause I am a little old fashioned; I do not 
care to wear the latest style in clothes or hair 
dress. I’ve never been a flirt or a vamp. 

I am eighteen years old, and my husband 
is twenty-one. We have been married over 
six months, and our only visitors have been our 
relatives. None of hubby’s friends ever come 
to visit us. I wonder why? 

I love horses and horseback riding. Horses 
are my hobby, though I love most any other 
outdoor sport. I rode for nearly two years 
steady and that’s how I acquired my name. 

I would like to make friends with The 
Hollow Tree Gang. RIbI NG OLLIE.

Dear Miss Rivers: I want to correct the 
impression as given out by Miss Stewart of 
Yakima, Washington, that that city “has more 
than its share of wind.” In the first place, 
the country around Yakima isn’t level enough 
for high-speed winds. Secondly, it is sur- 
rounded by high hills on every side, thus creat-

conditions. Joun R. CLARK.

Dear GANG: I live in the heart of the 
very largest lead and zinc fields in the world; 
there are thousands of men employed in this 
district. If you leave Joplin, Missouri, about 
four o’clock any afternoon and drive over the 
concrete highway to Baxter Springs, Kansas, 
and on into Picher, Oklahoma, you see a sight 
to remember. Several hundred men make the 
trip twice daily from even Webb City, Mis- 
souri, into the Oklahoma zinc fields. It takes 
rather a skilled driver to wind his way through 
the mass of cars taking the miners home. In- 
terurban cars run from Joplin to Picher every 
thirty minutes. 

The small mining town I live in adjoins the 
State of Kansas; it’s a dime car fare to Baxter 
Springs; Miami, the county seat, is only about 
fourteen miles, and for forty-five cents you 
can go to Joplin, Missouri. ö 

Would like to hear from A Prospectoress 
and learn what she is prospecting for, from 
women in mountainous States, and those in- 
terested in handwork. Am never lonesome for 
the want of seeing people and hearing things, 
but I love to receive and answer letters. 

Mrs. M. FUSSELL.

Dear Miss Rivers: I am writing to see if 
any one would write to me up here in North- 
western Canada. I am out here in the woods 
fifty-seven miles from civilization. I have 
been trapping once in a while for twelve years 
with an old trapper now seventy-eight years 
of age. All the fun I have is in hunting and 
with my tame black bear, now seven months 
old; he gets rough once in a while. 

I get my mail twice a month brought up 
here by dog team by another prospector. I'd 
like to have some letters, and will answer 
questions about mountains and woods. 


Care of The Tree. S. A. B. 


Dear Miss Rivers: Is there any of your 
members between the ages of twenty-five and 
thirty-five with a car, who'd like to travel 
this summer and camp out? I am looking 
for a partner who'd be a square shooter and 
likes hunting and fishing. My wife has heart 


138 


trouble and I want to travel toward the South; 
Texas is my destination. I have had lots of 
sickness, have been unfortunate in different 
ways, and am now working for wages; I 
can’t seem to get ahead enough to get an out- 
fit to travel. But I would furnish the grub- 
stake and bear half the expenses with any 
one who wants to travel this way. 

There are four in our family—wife, two 
little boys, and me. My wife is a splendid 
cook and likes to camp out; we like to have 
a good time in a clean way and are respectable 
people. 

If this interests any one drop me a line in 
care of The Tree. Would be glad to hear 
from Gangsters who know Texas and Okla- 
homa well. H. H. P., or Montana. 


Dear Miss Rivers ann Ganc: Rap! Rap! 
Open the door to a girl wanderer; let me sit 
by the camp fire and rest. I’m not a cowgirl, 
homesteader, or anything romantic, just a trav- 
eling saleslady, who, in spite of seeing “oodles” 
of people every day, gets powerful lonesome. 
Have traveled through almost all the States, 
Canada, China, and a few more places; have 
had my share of hard knocks, but have man- 
aged to come out on top. 

` Have you ever, after a day’s work, gone to 
your room to rest, only to hear the toot-toot 
of automobiles, the rumble of street cars, the 
shrill cries of the “newsies,” and then finally 
give up in despair, taken the car and driven 
out into the country, parked on seme hill where 
you could breathe the wonderful fresh air and 
hear the cows lowing in some distant pasture? 
I have, many a time. 

I enjoy camping, fishing, hunting, exploring, 
and everything pertaining to nature. I often 
envy the people on the farm; they all seem so 
contented and happy. Some day I mean to live 
on a farm, too. We poor humans, as a rule, 
unfortunately have too little “play time” and 
too much overtime.” 

Would be happy to hear from you, one and 
all, in America, Europe, Africa, India, every- 
where. Will. answer all letters I may receive 
and also answer questions concerning any for- 
eign land I have been in. Now, please, folks, 
don’t forget. GIRL WANDERER. 

Care of The Tree.

Dear Hottow Tree: After all my travels 
over the Western States and Canada I have 
decided to use my homestead right and get 
down to business. I would appreciate some in- 
formation from Gangsters regarding good lo- 
‘cations for stock raising only. I am not in- 
terested in agriculture, only in seeding for hay 
if conditions would permit and require same. 
Washington and Oregon are the States I want 
to hear about. 

„ Oakland is California’s third largest city 
and, I believe, occupies one of the choicest 
places along the Pacific Ocean; it is a won- 
derful industrial city. The tourist is conven- 
iently near the prominent pleasure resorts and 
the health seeker will find an ideal climate in 
which to recuperate. California has many dif- 
ferent climates and Oakland has one of the 
best. I was at Truckee, California, in the 
Sierras in October, 1925, and witnessed three 
snowstorms, one of them four inches deep. 
The altitude is seven thousand feet there. 

Mr. M. V. Webber, I see you have chosen 
Washington as your home, and I want to say 
I have never seen the scenic attractions to 
equal those of your State. 

I will answer every letter, and no doubt will 
see some of The Gang in Washington and Ore- 
gon. While I’m living a city life at present 
don’t think me a tenderfoot, for I detest city 
life, especially. for the working man.

Dear Miss Rivers: I work for the Den- 
ver and Salt Lake Railway in the heart of the 
Rockies and have a passenger run from State 
Bridge to Craig, Colorado. We have the most 
wonderful scenery of any place. The main 
industry here is coal mining and cattle rais- 
ing; oil is just coming to the front; we have 
a train of oil a day. The end of the railroad 
is Craig, but expect to start to build to Salt 
Lake City soon. We have the highest broad- 
gauge railway in the world, being 11,660 feet 
high, also we are completing the longest rail- 
way tunnel, six and a quarter miles; when 
done it will save twenty-four miles and a four- 
per-cent grade. Epwarp SULLIVAN, 

General Delivery, Craig, Colo.

WESTERN TRAILS—NATIONAL OLD TRAILS ROAD 


HERE is probably no trail on 

this continent that evokes more 

memories of the early settlers 
and their movements than does the Na- 
tional Old Trails Road, the first of the 
transcontinental highways. This trail 
follows the old National Pike, conceived 
by George Washington in 1784 and built 
by the government in 1806, from Wash- 
ington to St. Louis. From here to Old 
Franklin, across the river from Boone- 
ville, it follows the Boone Lick Trail 
used by Daniel Boone. From Old 
Franklin via Kansas City to Santa Fe, 
New Mexico, it follows the Santa Fe 
Trail, on which took place many fierce 
encounters between marauding Indians 
and the whites who were making the 
trip in covered wagons. Leaving Santa 
Fe, the route runs to the Pacific coast, 
following the Old Padres’ Trail trav- 
eled by the Spanish missionaries to and 
from their visits to the missions. 


From its- junction with the Old Na- 
tional Pike at Washington, District of 
Columbia, to its termination at Los An- 
geles, this road covers 3,096 miles, pass- 
ing through twelve States, six east and 
six west of the Mississippi. The first 
iron bridge built in America, the eighty- 
foot single arch across Dunlap's Creek, 
in Brownsville, Pennsylvania, between 
Uniontown and Wheeling, West Vir- 
ginia, still carries the traffic of the fa- 
mous National Pike. This structure 
was completed in 1839, and was crossed 
by a constant stream of horse-drawn 
travel for more than half a century 
after the coming of the railways. New 
an endless procession of trucks and 
automobiles, of all makes and styles 
and ages, may be seen every day racing 
across the sturdy old bridge, carrying 
both freight and passengers in heavier 
loads than were ever dreamed of by 
its far-seeing designer, Captain Rich- 


140 


ard Delafield, United States army en- 
gineer corps, who built, not for his own 
day alone, but also for posterity. 

George Washington personally lo- 
cated much of the route of the National 
Pike, first known as the Cumberland 
Road, across the mountains, but it was 
not until 1818 that construction was 
undertaken in earnest. It was a project 
of the Federal Government and is said 
to be the longest paved road in the 
world. 

Kansas City, Missouri, is the western 
gate of the National Old Trails Road. 
From here it runs to Los Angeles, a 
length of 1, 886 miles, and is parallel to 
the Santa Fe Railway nearly the whole 
of the route, so that the motorist is 
never very far from some town at 
which he can make a stop, secure sup- 
plies, make repairs, replenish the feed 
bag, or put up at an hotel or camp for 
as long as he wants to linger around. 

Going west from Kansas, the trail 
enters Colorado, traversing a region full 
of historic interest and wonderful scenic 
beauties. Eight miles west of Lamar, 
on the south side of the river, is the 
site of an old Indian village, and at Las 
Animas one may visit the old head- 
quarters of Kit Carson. The first per- 
manent settlement in Colorado was near 
Las Animas, and from this locality, in 
1806, Lieutenant Zebulon Pike is said 
to have first seen Pike’s Peak. Fifty 
years ago, Las Animas was noted for 
cattle round-ups from northern Texas, 
Indian Territory, and Kansas, also for 
buffalo-meat shipments, and both Buf- 
falo Bill and Kit Carson made their 
headquarters here for several years.. 

At La Junta the repair shops of. the 
Santa Fe Railway maintain an army of 
workers, and there are many agricul- 
tural opportunities in this section, sugar 
beet, alfalfa, cantaloupe, and stock rais- 
ing being among the successful ven- 
tures. Four irrigating systems supply 
water to farmers in this vicinity, and 
those who are looking around for a new 


WESTERN STORY MAGAZINE 


location will find many desirable spots 
in this favored part of the world. 

From La Junta the trail goes south- 
west to Trinidad. Thence it runs past 
several coal-mining camps and ascends 
the scenic road built by convicts of the 
Colorado prisons over the Raton Pass, 
from the summit of which a very fine 
view of the Rocky Mountains may be 
had. The peaks of the Dos Hermanos 
are visible to the west and to the north- 
east Fisher’s Peak, 10,000 feet high and 
with a curious flat top, may be seen. 
From the summit the road drops rap- 
idly across the New Mexico line into 
the town of Raton, the gate city of the 
sunshine State. 

This is one of New Mexico’s most 
attractive cities and is situated where 
the Raton Range of mountains unfolds 
the beautiful scenery of northern New 
Mexico. It is the center of immense 
coal fields with a big pay roll and has 
a large agricultural area. It also com- 
mands the entry to great mineral, tim- 
ber, and irrigated districts in north-cen- 
tral New Mexico. 


A BUDDY TO GO WEST 

Dear Mr. Norru: I am looking for a 
buddy, about my own age—eighteen, as I am 
planning to go West and want some one to go 
with me. I have small means to get there. 
If we had an opportunity for a job, I would 
want to stay. TERRY SWIFT. 

100 Clark Street, Evansville, Ind. 


This shoe-string proposition of trav- 
eling West is pretty risky, and I don’t 
recommend it at all. However, some 
will try it to be convinced, and perhaps 
this correspondent will find a buddy 
among my readers. 


WOULD BE A VAQUERO 
Dear Mr. NortH: I have heard rumors of 
Swift & Company sending riders to South 
America ‘under a three-year contract, and 
should like to know particulars. 
ARTHUR M. McComser. 


I do not know how this story got 
about, but I do know that I receive 
fully half a dozen letters similar to the 


WHERE TO GO AND HOW TO GET THERE 


above almost every day. No; Swift 
and the other big packers do not send 
cowboys or vaqueros to South America. 
There are plenty of good hands down 
there to take care of the cattle on the 
pampas, and there is no occasion to 
import cow-punchers from the States. 
It is only in the case of certain em- 
ployees who are particularly qualified 
to do work calling for a high degree of 


141 


skill and experience that these com- 
panies send men abroad. Frequently 
such men have been in the employ of 
the firms for several years and are par- 
ticularly valuable to the business by vir- 
tue of their long service. I am informed 
that the Swifts receive similar inquiries 
in large numbers, so it would appear 
that this erroneous report is pretty 
widespread. 


FATE OF BIG RANCH UNCERTAIN 
ON E of the remaining big ranch properties in California is that known as 

Rancho Santa Margarita, a vast ‘holding comprising some two. hundred and 
sixty thousand acres, situated about midway between Los Angeles and San Diego. 
For about twenty-five miles, the great ranch borders the sea. Back in the interior, 
on its wild mountain ranges is to be seen a picturesque survival of the life of 
old Spanish California. Here vaqueros still ride herd and round up the cattle 
and live the simple life of an old-time cow-puncher. 

The public knows little of this huge ranch, as it is guarded against trespassing 
and has few visitors. It is made up of old Spanish land grants, known as 
Trabuco, Viejo, and Santa Margarita y Las Flores. In the ’50s, these grants 
came into the possession of Juan Forster, a celebrated character of that era. 
When Forster died, the ranch went to his son, who preferred the bright lights 
of San Francisco to taking care of his business interests. The result was that 
the property was mortgaged; the bank foreclosed when payment was defaulted, 
and the ranch fell into the hands of a group of wealthy men, Flood, Fair, Macklin 
and O’Brien, who had made fortunes from the Comstock Lode in Nevada. These 
new owners sent Richard O’Neill to look after their ranch interests, and he 
managed the property so well that he was later given a half interest in Santa 
Margarita. On the death of O’Neill and Flood, their respective interests in the 
ranch descended to their sons, Jerome O’Neill and James Flood, Jr. These men 
have consistently held the ranch intact and have allowed their cattle to range as 
in the old days. 

By a strange coincidence, both men died during the early part of the present 
year, and the disposition of the Rancho Santa Margarita has become a matter 
of considerable speculation. The famous old ranch has much to recommend it 
to lovers of the West. The ranch house is a picturesque building, with the date 
1828 chiseled on its rock doorstep. Its adobe walls, nearly a century old, contain 
a priceless collection of relics and curios of early Spanish ‘California, as well as 
Spanish antiques, so that besides being a wonderful home, the ranch house is 
also a private museum of exceptional interest.

What’s the Fate of a Dare-devil Like This? 


Here’s Lew Melody. 
At the tender age of fourteen he shows up in the little town half dead from 
“knife wounds, the result of a brawl with Mexicans. 

Next winter he goes back and they find a Mexican with his throat cut. 

Then a series of fights and Lew Melody falls in love with beautiful “Sandy” 
Furnival, kills the man who insults her, wounds the sheriff, is hunted for his 
life and---— 

But you must read of Lew and his fate for vourself in 


The Black Signal 


A Western Story 
: By DAVID MANNING 


The author introduces you to a character whom you will long remember—the 
daring, gray-eyed, always-fascinating Lew Melody, feared of men, beloved of 
women. Mr. Manning tells his adventures in a galloping, breakneck manner 
that keeps you sitting on the edge of your chair. 

Ask for “The Black Signal” at your dealer's to-day. And at the same time 
ask to see the complete line of Chelsea House Popular Copyrights of which this 
smashing book is a sample. 

Chelsea House novels have never appeared between book covers before. They 
are new, exciting, the best of modern fiction.
